	Apache Kafka
	=============
	Apache Kafka is a tool that helps different applications send and receive data. It is used to transfer data between systems quickly and in real-time.
	
	Messaging System : 	A messaging system is a simple exchange of messages between two or more persons, devices, etc. 
						A publish-subscribe messaging system allows a sender to send/write the message and a receiver to read that message. 
						In Apache Kafka, a sender is known as a producer who publishes messages, and a receiver is known as a consumer who consumes that message by subscribing it.

	
	Streaming Process : A streaming process means handling data continuously as it arrives, without waiting.
						Different systems can process data at the same time (in parallel), making things faster.
						ex:  Netflix and Amazon Prime
	Kafka key Concepts 
	==================

	1. Producer: A producer is an application or service that sends data (messages or events) to a Kafka topic.
	
	2. Topic: A topic is a category or feed name where messages are published by producers and read by consumers. Topics help organize messages.
	
	3. Partition: Each topic can be divided into partitions. Partitions allow Kafka to scale horizontally and process data in parallel.
	
		Note : Scale horizontally means adding more machines or nodes to a system to handle increased load, instead of upgrading a single machine (which is called scaling vertically).

	4. Offset: An offset is a unique identifier assigned to each message in a partition. It helps keep track of the read position for consumers.
	
	5. Consumer: A consumer is an application or service that reads data from a Kafka topic.
	
	6. Consumer Group: A consumer group is a group of consumers that work together to consume messages from a topic. Each message is delivered to only one consumer in the group.
	
	7. Broker: A broker is a Kafka server that stores messages and serves them to consumers. Kafka clusters usually have multiple brokers.
	
	8. Cluster: Kafka Cluster is a group of Kafka brokers(servers) working together to manage and distribute data via topics. A Cluster is a collection of brokers.
	
	9. ZooKeeper: ZooKeeper was used for managing Kafka’s cluster metadata and coordination. Starting from Kafka version 3.0, it is optional and can be replaced with Kafka’s built-in KRaft mode.


	```
	+-----------+          +--------+          +----------------+
	| Producer  |  ----->  | Topic  |  ----->  | Partition(s)   |
	+-----------+          +--------+          +----------------+
												   |
												   v
											  +---------+
											  | Offset  |
											  +---------+

													|
													v
	+------------+       +----------------+     +-------------+
	| Consumer   | <---- | Consumer Group | <-- | Partition(s)|
	+------------+       +----------------+     +-------------+

													^
													|
											 +--------------+
											 | Kafka Broker |
											 +--------------+

													^
													|
											 +--------------+
											 | Kafka Cluster|
											 +--------------+

													^
													|
							 +------------------------------------------+
							 | ZooKeeper (or KRaft in Kafka 3.0+)       |
							 +------------------------------------------+
	```
	
	This diagram outlines the flow from **Producer** to **Topic**, how data is stored in **Partitions** with **Offsets**, how **Consumers** (in **Consumer Groups**) read that data, and how everything is managed by **Brokers** in a **Cluster**, coordinated via **ZooKeeper or KRaft**.
	
	JUNIT
	========
	It is an open-source testing framework for java programmers. The java programmer can create test cases and test his/her own code.

	It is one of the unit testing framework. Current version is junit 4.

	To perform unit testing, we need to create test cases. The unit test case is a code which ensures that the program logic works as expected.

	The org.junit package contains many interfaces and classes for junit testing such as Assert, Test, Before, After etc.



	1) Manual Testing
	If you execute the test cases manually without any tool support, it is known as manual testing. It is time consuming and less reliable.

	2) Automated Testing
	If you execute the test cases by tool support, it is known as automated testing. It is fast and more reliable. script example : Selenium WebDriver


	Annotations for Junit testing
	-------------------------------
	@Test annotation specifies that method is the test method.

	@Test(timeout=1000) annotation specifies that method will be failed if it takes longer than 1000 milliseconds (1 second).

	@BeforeClass annotation specifies that method will be invoked only once, before starting all the tests.

	@Before annotation specifies that method will be invoked before each test.

	@After annotation specifies that method will be invoked after each test.

	@AfterClass annotation specifies that method will be invoked only once, after finishing all the tests.



	Assert class
	-------------
	The org.junit.Assert class provides methods to assert the program logic.

	The common methods of Assert class are as follows:

	void assertEquals(boolean expected,boolean actual): checks that two primitives/objects are equal. It is overloaded.
	void assertTrue(boolean condition): checks that a condition is true.
	void assertFalse(boolean condition): checks that a condition is false.
	void assertNull(Object obj): checks that object is null.
	void assertNotNull(Object obj): checks that object is not null.
	
	Example: JUnit Annotations with Test Case Example and Use Case Comments
	-----------------------------------------------------------------------

	import org.junit.jupiter.api.*;
	import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.*;

	public class ExampleTest {

		// @BeforeAll: This method is executed once before any test methods are run. 
		// Use case: Typically used to set up expensive or shared resources (e.g., database connections) that are required by all tests.
		@BeforeAll
		public static void beforeAllTests() {
			System.out.println("Before all tests - @BeforeAll");
		}

		// @BeforeEach: This method is executed before each individual test method is run.
		// Use case: Ideal for initializing common resources that need to be set up before each test (e.g., mocking dependencies, initializing objects).
		@BeforeEach
		public void beforeEachTest() {
			System.out.println("Before each test - @BeforeEach");
		}

		// @Test(timeout = 1000): This annotation indicates that the test method will fail if it takes longer than the specified time (in this case, 1000 milliseconds or 1 second) to execute.
		// Use case: Useful for ensuring that your test method completes in a reasonable amount of time. Can be used for performance testing or to avoid tests that hang indefinitely.
		@Test(timeout = 1000) // Test will fail if execution takes more than 1 second
		public void testMethod1() throws InterruptedException {
			System.out.println("Running testMethod1 - @Test(timeout=1000)");
			// Simulating some test logic that takes less than 1000ms
			Thread.sleep(500); // Sleep for 500ms
			assertTrue(true);  // This will pass as it is always true
		}

		// @Test: This annotation marks a method as a test case.
		// Use case: Used to mark the actual test methods that you want to execute. It is the core annotation for running individual unit tests.
		@Test
		public void testMethod2() {
			System.out.println("Running testMethod2 - @Test");
			// Basic test case that checks if 2 + 3 equals 5
			assertEquals(5, 2 + 3); // This will pass as the result is 5
		}

		// @AfterEach: This method is executed after each individual test method completes.
		// Use case: Often used for cleanup activities, like resetting the state of a system or releasing resources (e.g., closing a database connection).
		@AfterEach
		public void afterEachTest() {
			System.out.println("After each test - @AfterEach");
		}

		// @AfterAll: This method is executed once after all the test methods have been executed.
		// Use case: Typically used for cleanup activities that need to happen once after all tests have completed, such as closing global resources or freeing up memory.
		@AfterAll
		public static void afterAllTests() {
			System.out.println("After all tests - @AfterAll");
		}
	}

	---

	### ✅ JUnit 4 Annotations (older version)

	| Annotation         | Description                                                                 |
	|--------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------|
	| `@Test`            | Marks a method as a test.                                                   |
	| `@Test(timeout=...)` | Fails if test takes longer than the specified time.                       |
	| `@BeforeClass`     | Runs **once** before all tests. Method must be `static`.                   |
	| `@Before`          | Runs **before each** test.                                                  |
	| `@After`           | Runs **after each** test.                                                   |
	| `@AfterClass`      | Runs **once** after all tests. Method must be `static`.                    |

	These are from **JUnit 4**, and `Assert` class methods like `assertEquals`, `assertTrue` are used with **static imports**.

	---

	### ✅ JUnit 5 Annotations (latest version, also known as Jupiter)

	| Annotation         | Description                                                                 |
	|--------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------|
	| `@Test`            | Marks a method as a test. No `timeout` parameter in this version.           |
	| `@BeforeAll`       | Runs once before all tests. Must be `static` unless using `@TestInstance`.  |
	| `@BeforeEach`      | Runs before each test.                                                       |
	| `@AfterEach`       | Runs after each test.                                                        |
	| `@AfterAll`        | Runs once after all tests. Must be `static` unless using `@TestInstance`.   |

	JUnit 5 uses `org.junit.jupiter.api.*` and methods from `org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions`.

	⚠️ **Important:**  
	- `@Test(timeout = 1000)` **doesn't work in JUnit 5**. Instead, use:
	  ```java
	  @Test
	  void testWithTimeout() {
		  assertTimeout(Duration.ofMillis(1000), () -> {
			  // test logic here
		  });
	  }
	  ```

	---

	### Summary:

	| Feature                      | JUnit 4                     | JUnit 5 (Jupiter)                |
	|-----------------------------|-----------------------------|----------------------------------|
	| Test method annotation      | `@Test`                     | `@Test`                          |
	| Timeout                     | `@Test(timeout=1000)`       | `assertTimeout(Duration.ofMillis(1000), ...)` |
	| Before all tests            | `@BeforeClass`              | `@BeforeAll`                     |
	| Before each test            | `@Before`                   | `@BeforeEach`                    |
	| After each test             | `@After`                    | `@AfterEach`                     |
	| After all tests             | `@AfterClass`               | `@AfterAll`                      |
	| Assertions class            | `org.junit.Assert`          | `org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions` |

	---


	MOCKITO TESTING 
	================


	### **Theory: Mockito and Unit Testing**

	**What is Mockito?**
	Mockito is a Java-based mocking framework that helps in creating mock objects for unit testing. It is primarily used to isolate unit tests from external dependencies (such as databases, APIs, etc.) to test the business logic in isolation.

	**Why Mockito?**
	- Allows testing without interacting with real dependencies (like databases).
	- It makes unit tests more focused, clear, and easy to maintain.
	- Verifies that specific methods are called or not during a test.

	**Key Methods in Mockito:**
	- `mock(Class<T> classToMock)`: Creates a mock object of the given class.
	- `when(...).thenReturn(...)`: Stubs a method to return a specific value when called.
	- `verify(...)`: Verifies if a method was called.
	- `@Mock`: Used to declare mock objects that Mockito will create for you.
	- `@InjectMocks`: Automatically injects mock objects into the class under test.
	- `@ExtendWith(MockitoExtension.class)`: Used to integrate Mockito with JUnit 5.

	### **Example: Employee Management in Spring Boot**

	This is a simple employee management example, with `Employee` having an embedded `Address` class.

	---

	#### **Model: Employee and Address Classes**

	```java
	@Entity
	public class Employee {
		@Id
		@GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
		private Long id;
		private String name;
		private String role;

		@Embedded
		private Address address;  // Address is embedded as part of Employee

		// Getters and setters
	}

	@Embeddable
	public class Address {
		private String street;
		private String city;
		private String zip;

		// Getters and setters
	}
	```

	---

	#### **Repository: EmployeeRepository**

	```java
	@Repository
	public interface EmployeeRepository extends JpaRepository<Employee, Long> {
		Optional<Employee> findByName(String name);  // Custom query to find by name
	}
	```

	---

	#### **Service: EmployeeService**

	```java
	@Service
	public class EmployeeService {

		@Autowired
		private EmployeeRepository employeeRepository;

		public Employee getEmployeeById(Long id) {
			return employeeRepository.findById(id).orElseThrow(() -> new RuntimeException("Employee not found"));
		}

		public Employee saveEmployee(Employee employee) {
			return employeeRepository.save(employee);
		}

		public Employee updateEmployee(Long id, Employee updatedEmployee) {
			Employee existingEmployee = getEmployeeById(id);
			existingEmployee.setName(updatedEmployee.getName());
			existingEmployee.setRole(updatedEmployee.getRole());
			existingEmployee.setAddress(updatedEmployee.getAddress());
			return employeeRepository.save(existingEmployee);
		}

		public void deleteEmployee(Long id) {
			employeeRepository.deleteById(id);
		}
	}
	```

	---

	#### **Controller: EmployeeController**

	```java
	@RestController
	@RequestMapping("/employees")
	public class EmployeeController {

		@Autowired
		private EmployeeService employeeService;

		@GetMapping("/{id}")
		public ResponseEntity<Employee> getEmployeeById(@PathVariable Long id) {
			Employee employee = employeeService.getEmployeeById(id);
			return ResponseEntity.ok(employee);
		}

		@PostMapping("/")
		public ResponseEntity<Employee> createEmployee(@RequestBody Employee employee) {
			Employee savedEmployee = employeeService.saveEmployee(employee);
			return ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.CREATED).body(savedEmployee);
		}

		@PutMapping("/{id}")
		public ResponseEntity<Employee> updateEmployee(@PathVariable Long id, @RequestBody Employee updatedEmployee) {
			Employee employee = employeeService.updateEmployee(id, updatedEmployee);
			return ResponseEntity.ok(employee);
		}

		@DeleteMapping("/{id}")
		public ResponseEntity<Void> deleteEmployee(@PathVariable Long id) {
			employeeService.deleteEmployee(id);
			return ResponseEntity.noContent().build();
		}
	}
	```

	---

	### **Service Test Class: EmployeeServiceTest**

	**Explanation of Test Cases:**

	We will use Mockito to mock the `EmployeeRepository` and test the `EmployeeService` methods.

	```java
	@ExtendWith(MockitoExtension.class)
	public class EmployeeServiceTest {

		@Mock
		private EmployeeRepository employeeRepository;

		@InjectMocks
		private EmployeeService employeeService;

		@Test
		public void testSaveEmployee() {
			Employee employee = new Employee();
			employee.setName("John Doe");
			employee.setRole("Developer");
			employee.setAddress(new Address("123 Main St", "City", "12345"));

			when(employeeRepository.save(any(Employee.class))).thenReturn(employee);

			Employee savedEmployee = employeeService.saveEmployee(employee);

			assertNotNull(savedEmployee);
			assertEquals("John Doe", savedEmployee.getName());
			verify(employeeRepository, times(1)).save(employee);  // Verify that save was called once
		}

		@Test
		public void testGetEmployeeById() {
			Employee employee = new Employee();
			employee.setId(1L);
			employee.setName("John Doe");
			employee.setRole("Developer");

			when(employeeRepository.findById(1L)).thenReturn(Optional.of(employee));

			Employee fetchedEmployee = employeeService.getEmployeeById(1L);

			assertNotNull(fetchedEmployee);
			assertEquals("John Doe", fetchedEmployee.getName());
			verify(employeeRepository, times(1)).findById(1L);  // Verify that findById was called once
		}

		@Test
		public void testUpdateEmployee() {
			Employee existingEmployee = new Employee();
			existingEmployee.setId(1L);
			existingEmployee.setName("John Doe");
			existingEmployee.setRole("Developer");

			Employee updatedEmployee = new Employee();
			updatedEmployee.setName("John Doe Updated");
			updatedEmployee.setRole("Senior Developer");

			when(employeeRepository.findById(1L)).thenReturn(Optional.of(existingEmployee));
			when(employeeRepository.save(any(Employee.class))).thenReturn(updatedEmployee);

			Employee updatedEmp = employeeService.updateEmployee(1L, updatedEmployee);

			assertNotNull(updatedEmp);
			assertEquals("John Doe Updated", updatedEmp.getName());
			assertEquals("Senior Developer", updatedEmp.getRole());
			verify(employeeRepository, times(1)).save(updatedEmployee);  // Verify that save was called once
		}

		@Test
		public void testDeleteEmployee() {
			Long employeeId = 1L;
			// Mock the deleteById method to do nothing (no actual deletion)
			doNothing().when(employeeRepository).deleteById(employeeId);

			// Call the deleteEmployee method
			employeeService.deleteEmployee(employeeId);

			// Verify that deleteById was called once
			verify(employeeRepository, times(1)).deleteById(employeeId);
		}
	}
	```

		---
	import static org.mockito.Mockito.*;
	import static org.springframework.test.web.servlet.result.MockMvcResultMatchers.*;

	import com.fasterxml.jackson.databind.ObjectMapper;
	import org.junit.jupiter.api.BeforeEach;
	import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
	import org.mockito.InjectMocks;
	import org.mockito.Mock;
	import org.springframework.beans.factory.annotation.Autowired;
	import org.springframework.boot.test.autoconfigure.web.servlet.WebMvcTest;
	import org.springframework.test.web.servlet.MockMvc;
	import org.springframework.http.MediaType;

	@WebMvcTest(EmployeeController.class)  // Load only the controller for testing
	public class EmployeeControllerTest {

		@Autowired
		private MockMvc mockMvc;  // Used to perform HTTP requests in tests

		@Mock
		private EmployeeService employeeService;  // Mock the service layer

		@InjectMocks
		private EmployeeController employeeController;  // Inject the mocked service into the controller

		@Autowired
		private ObjectMapper objectMapper;  // To serialize the employee object

		private Employee employee;

		// @BeforeEach: This ensures that the setup() method is executed before each test method runs.
		// The method initializes the employee object with a mock Employee instance that will be used across all tests in the class. This is helpful when you need to set up some common data or objects that all tests can share.
		@BeforeEach
		public void setup() {
			// Setup a mock Employee object for testing
			employee = new Employee(1L, "John Doe", "Software Engineer", 50000);
		}

		@Test
		public void testGetEmployeeById() throws Exception {
			// Mocking the service method
			when(employeeService.getEmployeeById(1L)).thenReturn(employee);

			// Perform a GET request to /employees/{id}
			mockMvc.perform(get("/employees/{id}", 1L))
					.andExpect(status().isOk())
					.andExpect(jsonPath("$.id").value(1L))
					.andExpect(jsonPath("$.name").value("John Doe"))
					.andExpect(jsonPath("$.jobTitle").value("Software Engineer"));
		}

		@Test
		public void testCreateEmployee() throws Exception {
			// Mocking the service method for save
			when(employeeService.saveEmployee(any(Employee.class))).thenReturn(employee);

			// Perform a POST request to /employees/
			mockMvc.perform(post("/employees/")
							.contentType(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
							.content(objectMapper.writeValueAsString(employee)))
					.andExpect(status().isCreated())
					.andExpect(jsonPath("$.id").value(1L))
					.andExpect(jsonPath("$.name").value("John Doe"));
		}

		@Test
		public void testUpdateEmployee() throws Exception {
			// Mocking the service method for update
			when(employeeService.updateEmployee(eq(1L), any(Employee.class))).thenReturn(employee);

			// Perform a PUT request to /employees/{id}
			mockMvc.perform(put("/employees/{id}", 1L)
							.contentType(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
							.content(objectMapper.writeValueAsString(employee)))
					.andExpect(status().isOk())
					.andExpect(jsonPath("$.id").value(1L))
					.andExpect(jsonPath("$.name").value("John Doe"));
		}

		@Test
		public void testDeleteEmployee() throws Exception {
			// Mocking the service method for delete
			doNothing().when(employeeService).deleteEmployee(1L);

			// Perform a DELETE request to /employees/{id}
			mockMvc.perform(delete("/employees/{id}", 1L))
					.andExpect(status().isNoContent());
		}
	}


	StateFarm B2B Portal 
	=============================

	The State Farm B2B Portal is a centralized platform designed to streamline interactions between State Farm and its business partners.  
	Instead of handling everything over phone or mail, partners use this website to do things faster and online.


	Select Service  
	---------------------
	Auto repair facilities can manage claims, create assignments, and access performance reports.  
	A car repair shop can accept a vehicle claim and update repair progress.  
	This helps repair shops stay connected with State Farm claims.

	Medical Billing  
	--------------------
	Healthcare providers can submit medical claims related to auto accidents and access payment information.  
	A hospital or clinic can send bills directly to State Farm for accident treatment.  
	Helps doctors and clinics get paid for treating accident victims.

	 Electronic Payments  
	-------------------------
	Partners can set up and manage Electronic Funds Transfer (EFT) payments, view remittances, and access related forms.  
	Makes it easy to receive payments electronically.

	Suppliers Portal  
	-----------------------
	Vendors can engage with State Farm's procurement processes through the Coupa platform, managing orders, invoices, and supplier information.  
	A supplier can send an invoice and get paid quickly.  
	Manages orders and payments through Coupa.

	Home & Auto Lenders  
	-------------------------
	Financial institutions can verify insurance policies, manage escrow billing, and access mortgage payment systems.  
	A bank can check if a house has the right insurance.  
	Lenders can manage insurance data for escrow and home loans.

	---

	How It Works:
	-------------

	- It pulls claims data from State Farm’s internal systems (used by claims.statefarm.com).  
	- It works with finance tools that connect to agent systems and the employee portal (sfnet).  
	- It keeps everything secure and up-to-date across all platforms.

	---

	Advantages:

	- Saves time for both State Farm and its partners.  
	- Improves accuracy by avoiding manual errors.  
	- Speeds up processes – claims get processed, payments made, services delivered.  
	- Scales efficiently – thousands of partners can be handled smoothly.

	---


	Feature 				| Purpose 										| Used By
	Create Assignments 		| Assigns claims to repair shops 				| State Farm
	Remittance 				| Shows payment details for completed jobs 		| State Farm to Shop

	✅ 1. Select Service - Vehicle Claims for Repair Shops

	One Example How it works for one vendor
	========================================


	Select Service Use Case: Car Accident Repair Process

	Scenario:  
	A customer is involved in a car accident and initiates an auto claim with State Farm.

	Step-by-Step Breakdown of How a Repair Shop Uses the B2B Portal

	1. Claim Assignment

	- Customer Action:  
	  The customer files an auto claim via a State Farm agent or online.

	- State Farm Action:  
	  A claims adjuster reviews the claim.

	- Repair Shop Trigger:  
	  If the vehicle is drivable or towed to a Select Service shop, the shop receives the claim via the B2B portal.

	- Portal Action (Repair Shop):  
	  - Login to the B2B portal  
	  - Navigate to “Select Service” section  
	  - View list of newly assigned claims

	2. Claim Acceptance

	- Repair Shop Reviews:  
	  - Vehicle Info (VIN, Make, Model)  
	  - Customer Contact Info  
	  - Damage Details

	- Portal Action:  
	  - Click “Accept Claim” to begin the repair process

	3. Estimate Submission

	- Repair Shop Action:  
	  - Inspect the vehicle  
	  - Prepare cost estimate

	- Portal Action:  
	  - Upload estimate documents (PDF, Images)  
	  - Enter parts and labor cost  
	  - Submit estimate for State Farm review

	4. Status Updates

	- Repair Shop Status Options:  
	  - In Progress  
	  - Waiting on Parts  
	  - Ready for Pickup  
	  - Completed

	- Portal Action:  
	  - Update status in the B2B portal to keep both State Farm and the customer informed in real time

	5. Repair Completion and Billing

	- Repair Shop Final Steps:  
	  - Mark the claim as “Completed”  
	  - Submit final invoice (if required)  
	  - If EFT is set up, payment is processed automatically

	Example Portal View for a Repair Shop:

	Claim ID     | Vehicle      | Status             | Estimate | Last Updated  
	-------------|--------------|--------------------|----------|----------------  
	CLM12345     | 2019 Camry   | In Progress        | $2,340   | April 25, 2025  
	CLM12346     | 2021 Civic   | Estimate Submitted | $1,890   | April 24, 2025  

	Why the B2B Portal is Important for Repair Shops:

	- Eliminates the need for constant calls or emails to adjusters  
	- Enables real-time communication with State Farm  
	- Speeds up claim handling and repair process  
	- Integrated payment flow ensures quicker payouts

		JVM Memory Or Java Memory Management
	============================================

		1. Method area: In the method area, all class level information like class name, immediate parent class name, methods and variables information etc are stored, including static variables. 
		   There is only one method area per JVM, and it is a shared resource. 

		2. Heap area: The Heap Area is where all objects in a Java application are stored. It is one of the key components of memory management in the Java Virtual Machine (JVM). There is only one heap area for each JVM instance
		   All dynamically created objects, such as instances of classes, are allocated memory in the heap. The heap is shared by all threads in the JVM, meaning all threads can access and work with objects stored in the heap.
			
			The heap area is divided into two main generations to optimize memory management and garbage collection:
			i. Young Generation: The Young Generation is where new objects are initially created.
			ii.Old Generation: The Old Generation is for objects that have lived longer and survived multiple garbage collection cycles in the Young Generation.

			Minor GC handles garbage collection for the Young Generation, and major GC handles it for the Old Generation.

		3. Stack area: For every thread, JVM creates one run-time stack which is stored here. Every block of this stack is called activation record/stack frame which stores methods calls. 
		   All local variables of that method are stored in their corresponding frame. After a thread terminates, its run-time stack will be destroyed by JVM. It is not a shared resource.
		   (The stack area simply stores method calls and local variables per stack frame.)
		   
		4. PC Registers: Store address of current execution instruction of a thread. Obviously, each thread has separate PC Registers.

		5. Native method stacks: For every thread, a separate native stack is created. It stores native method information or it stores native method calls (e.g., methods written in C, C++).


		### Garbage Collection (GC)

		Garbage Collection is the process by which unused objects (no longer referenced) are removed from the heap to free up memory. 
		This prevents memory leaks and ensures the program doesn't run out of memory.

		#### Types of Garbage Collectors

		1. **Serial GC**  
		   - Single-threaded GC.
		   - Best for small applications with single-threaded execution.
		   - Example:  
			 Use `-XX:+UseSerialGC` to enable.

		2. **Parallel GC**  
		   - Multi-threaded GC for high-throughput.
		   - Best for multi-threaded applications.
		   - Example:  
			 Use `-XX:+UseParallelGC` to enable.

		3. **G1 GC (Garbage-First)**  
		   - Low-latency GC that aims for predictable pause times.
		   - Best for applications with large heaps.
		   - Example:  
			 Use `-XX:+UseG1GC` to enable.

		4. **ZGC (Z Garbage Collector)**  
		   - Designed for low-latency with large heaps.
		   - Aimed at applications where pause time must be minimal.
		   - Example:  
			 Use `-XX:+UseZGC` to enable.

		---

		### Garbage Collection Example

		```java
		public class GCDemo {
			public static void main(String[] args) {
				Person person = new Person("Alice");  // Object created in Heap
				person = null;  // Object is eligible for GC
				
				// Suggest to JVM to perform garbage collection
				System.gc();  
			}

			static class Person {
				String name;
				
				Person(String name) {
					this.name = name;
				}

				// This method is called when GC happens
				@Override
				protected void finalize() throws Throwable {
					System.out.println(name + " has been garbage collected!");
				}
			}
		}
		```

		- Explanation:  
		  In this example, we create an object `Person` and then set the reference to `null`. The object becomes eligible for garbage collection. We call `System.gc()` to suggest garbage collection. The `finalize()` method is called when the object is collected.

		### Conclusion

		Java's memory management and garbage collection processes ensure that your applications run smoothly without running out of memory. By understanding how memory is allocated in Method Area, Heap, Stack, etc., and how Garbage Collection works, you can optimize your application for better performance.
			
		
		✅ When Does `OutOfMemoryError` Occur in Spring Boot?
		------------------------------------------------------
		`OutOfMemoryError` in a Spring Boot application typically occurs when the **Java Virtual Machine (JVM)** runs out of memory. This can happen in several areas:

		#### Common Causes:

		1. **Heap Memory Exhaustion** – Too many or large objects kept in memory.
		2. **Memory Leaks** – Beans or objects are never garbage collected due to lingering references.
		3. **Unbounded Caching** – Using `Map`, `List`, or caches like `ConcurrentHashMap` without size limits.
		4. **Large File Uploads** – Uploading large files without proper limits.
		5. **Improper Thread Usage** – Too many threads created and not managed.
		6. **Database ResultSet** – Loading massive data into memory at once.

		---

		✅ **1. How to Check Memory Usage of a List**

		You can **estimate** how much memory a `List` (like `ArrayList`) takes using `Runtime`:

		```java
		import java.util.ArrayList;

		public class MemoryUsageExample {
			public static void main(String[] args) {
				Runtime runtime = Runtime.getRuntime();

				// Run GC before measuring
				runtime.gc();
				long before = runtime.totalMemory() - runtime.freeMemory();

				// Create a list
				ArrayList<String> list = new ArrayList<>();
				for (int i = 0; i < 10000; i++) {
					list.add("Item " + i);
				}

				runtime.gc(); // Suggest GC again
				long after = runtime.totalMemory() - runtime.freeMemory();

				System.out.println("Approx. memory used by list: " + (after - before) + " bytes");
			}
		}
		```

		> This gives a rough estimate of memory used by the list.

		---

		✅ **2. What Is the Default JVM Memory?**

		The default memory settings vary depending on the JVM and machine, but **typical defaults** are:

		| Memory Area            | Default Value                                    |
		| ---------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ |
		| **Initial Heap (Xms)** | \~256MB                                          |
		| **Max Heap (Xmx)**     | \~512MB or 1/4 of physical memory                |
		| **PermGen/Metaspace**  | \~64MB (PermGen in Java 7, Metaspace in Java 8+) |

		---

		### ✅ **Check Current JVM Memory Settings**

		You can print them like this:

		```java
		public class JvmMemoryCheck {
			public static void main(String[] args) {
				Runtime runtime = Runtime.getRuntime();

				System.out.println("Max Memory (Xmx): " + runtime.maxMemory() / (1024 * 1024) + " MB");
				System.out.println("Total Memory (Xms): " + runtime.totalMemory() / (1024 * 1024) + " MB");
				System.out.println("Free Memory: " + runtime.freeMemory() / (1024 * 1024) + " MB");
			}
		}
		```

		---

		### ✅ Optional: Set JVM Memory Manually

		Set heap size explicitly when running your Spring Boot app:

		```bash
		java -Xms512m -Xmx1024m -jar app.jar
		```

		Would you like a full example for measuring memory in a Spring Boot context?

		---

		✅ How to Resolve It in Spring Boot?

		1. **Increase JVM Heap Size**

		Update your run command or in your service configuration:

		```bash
		java -Xms512m -Xmx2g -jar your-springboot-app.jar
		```

		2. **Limit File Upload Size**

		Add in `application.properties`:

		```properties
		spring.servlet.multipart.max-file-size=10MB
		spring.servlet.multipart.max-request-size=10MB
		```

		3. **Use Pagination for Large DB Results**

		```java
		Page<User> users = userRepository.findAll(PageRequest.of(0, 100));
		```

		4. **Use Caching Wisely**

		If using `@Cacheable`, set limits or expiration using a cache provider like Caffeine or Redis.

		5. **Fix Memory Leaks**

		Use memory profilers like **VisualVM**, **Eclipse MAT**, or **JProfiler** to detect leaks. Ensure:

		* No unnecessary `@PostConstruct` objects held globally.
		* Beans are not unnecessarily `@Scope("prototype")`.

		6. **Use Stream Instead of Loading All**

		```java
		@Query("SELECT u FROM User u")
		Stream<User> streamAllUsers();  // Then close stream manually
		```

		7. **Tune Garbage Collection**

		Enable better GC for large applications:

		```bash
		-XX:+UseG1GC
		```

		8. **Profile and Monitor**

		Use **Actuator**, **Prometheus**, and **Grafana** to monitor memory usage:

		```xml
		<dependency>
		  <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
		  <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-actuator</artifactId>
		</dependency>
		```

		---

		### 🔍 Summary

		**OutOfMemoryError happens when your app consumes more memory than allocated.**
		Fix it by:

		* Tuning heap size
		* Managing object lifecycles
		* Limiting resource usage
		* Using profiling tools to find leaks


		
		### Multithreading in Java
	======================================================

		Multithreading is the ability of a CPU, or a single core in a multi-core processor, to execute multiple threads concurrently. Threads are lightweight processes that share the same memory space. 
		In Java, threads allow for parallel execution, improving the performance of applications that need to perform many tasks simultaneously.

		Let's break down the key concepts related to multithreading in Java:
		---
		### 1. **Basic Thread Concepts**

		#### **Thread:**
		A thread is a single unit of execution within a process. In Java, you can create threads in two main ways:
		1. By extending the `Thread` class.
		2. By implementing the `Runnable` interface.

		#### **volatile keyword:**
		The `volatile` keyword is used to indicate that a variable's value can be modified by multiple threads concurrently. It ensures that the latest value of a variable is always visible to all threads, and it prevents caching of the variable in registers.
		volatile tells the Java Virtual Machine (JVM) that a variable's value may be modified by multiple threads, and it should always read the variable from main memory, not from the thread's local cache.

		**Example:**
		```java
		public class VolatileExample {
			private volatile boolean flag = false;

			public void startThread() {
				Thread t = new Thread(() -> {
					while (!flag) {
						// Some work
					}
					System.out.println("Thread finished.");
				});
				t.start();
			}

			public void stopThread() {
				flag = true;  // Once flag is set, the other thread will stop.
			}
		}
		```

		#### **Race Conditions:**
		A race condition occurs when two or more threads try to change shared data at the same time. This can lead to unpredictable behavior. The solution is synchronization or other concurrency control mechanisms like locks.

		**Example of a Race Condition:**
		```java
		public class RaceConditionExample {
			private int counter = 0;

			public void increment() {
				counter++;
			}

			public int getCounter() {
				return counter;
			}

			public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
				RaceConditionExample example = new RaceConditionExample();
				Runnable task = () -> {
					for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {
						example.increment();
					}
				};

				Thread t1 = new Thread(task);
				Thread t2 = new Thread(task);

				t1.start();
				t2.start();

				t1.join();
				t2.join();

				System.out.println("Counter: " + example.getCounter());
			}
		}
		```
		In this example, `counter` is modified by two threads, and due to a race condition, the result is inconsistent.

		---

		### 2. **Thread Pools and Executors**

		In Java, creating a new thread every time you need to execute a task can be inefficient. Instead, **thread pools** can be used to manage a group of worker threads.

		#### **Executor Service:**
		The `Executor` framework in Java provides a higher-level replacement for the traditional approach of using `Thread` and `Runnable`. It allows you to manage a pool of threads and execute tasks asynchronously.

		**Executor Interface** is the root interface for the executor framework, and it has two main implementations:
		1. **ExecutorService:** Adds functionality to manage tasks and retrieve results.
		2. **ScheduledExecutorService:** Adds functionality to schedule tasks.

		#### **Creating an Executor Service:**
		```java
		import java.util.concurrent.ExecutorService;
		import java.util.concurrent.Executors;

		public class ExecutorServiceExample {
			public static void main(String[] args) {
				// Create a thread pool with 5 threads
				ExecutorService executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(5);
				
				// Submit tasks to the thread pool
				for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
					executor.submit(() -> {
						System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " is executing task");
					});
				}
				
				// Shut down the executor
				executor.shutdown();
			}
		}
		```

		In the above example, a **FixedThreadPool** is created, where 5 threads are available to execute 10 tasks. The `submit()` method is used to submit tasks, and `shutdown()` shuts down the executor service.

		#### **Future and Callable:**
		- **Callable**: Similar to `Runnable`, but it can return a result or throw an exception.
		- **Future**: Represents the result of an asynchronous computation. It can be used to check the status or retrieve the result.

		**Example with Callable and Future:**
		```java
		import java.util.concurrent.*;

		public class CallableFutureExample {
			public static void main(String[] args) throws ExecutionException, InterruptedException {
				ExecutorService executor = Executors.newSingleThreadExecutor();
				
				Callable<Integer> task = () -> {
					Thread.sleep(2000);
					return 123;
				};
				
				Future<Integer> future = executor.submit(task);
				
				// Do some other work
				System.out.println("Task submitted...");
				
				// Get the result of the task (this will block until the task finishes)
				System.out.println("Task result: " + future.get());
				
				executor.shutdown();
			}
		}
		```
		In this example, the `Callable` task returns a result, and `Future.get()` is used to retrieve the result once the task completes.

		---

		### 3. **Synchronization and Locks**

		In multithreaded programming, it is crucial to manage shared resources properly. This can be done using **synchronization** or **locks**.

		#### **Synchronized Methods/Blocks:**
		The `synchronized` keyword ensures that only one thread can access a method or block of code at a time.

		**Example of Synchronized Method:**
		```java
		public class SynchronizedExample {
			private int counter = 0;

			public synchronized void increment() {
				counter++;
			}

			public synchronized int getCounter() {
				return counter;
			}

			public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
				SynchronizedExample example = new SynchronizedExample();
				Runnable task = () -> {
					for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {
						example.increment();
					}
				};

				Thread t1 = new Thread(task);
				Thread t2 = new Thread(task);

				t1.start();
				t2.start();

				t1.join();
				t2.join();

				System.out.println("Counter: " + example.getCounter());
			}
		}
		```
		Here, the `increment()` method is synchronized, ensuring that only one thread can update the `counter` at a time, thus avoiding a race condition.

		#### **Locks:**
		Locks are more flexible and powerful than synchronized blocks. They provide additional features like the ability to try locking and unlocking explicitly, allowing for better control over thread execution.

		**Example using ReentrantLock:**
		```java
		import java.util.concurrent.locks.Lock;
		import java.util.concurrent.locks.ReentrantLock;

		public class LockExample {
			private int counter = 0;
			private Lock lock = new ReentrantLock();

			public void increment() {
				lock.lock();
				try {
					counter++;
				} finally {
					lock.unlock();
				}
			}

			public int getCounter() {
				return counter;
			}

			public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
				LockExample example = new LockExample();
				Runnable task = () -> {
					for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {
						example.increment();
					}
				};

				Thread t1 = new Thread(task);
				Thread t2 = new Thread(task);

				t1.start();
				t2.start();

				t1.join();
				t2.join();

				System.out.println("Counter: " + example.getCounter());
			}
		}
		```
		In this example, we use a `ReentrantLock` to ensure that the `increment()` method is executed safely by multiple threads. The `lock.lock()` ensures exclusive access, and `lock.unlock()` releases the lock.

		---

		### 4. **Thread Pooling**

		Using thread pools allows efficient management of threads. Instead of creating a new thread for each task, thread pools reuse a set of threads, which reduces the overhead of thread creation and destruction.

		**Example of Thread Pool Usage:**
		```java
		import java.util.concurrent.*;

		public class ThreadPoolExample {
			public static void main(String[] args) {
				// Create a thread pool with 3 threads
				ExecutorService pool = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(3);

				// Submit tasks to the pool
				for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
					pool.submit(() -> {
						System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " is executing task");
					});
				}

				pool.shutdown();
			}
		}
		```

		---

		### Summary

		1. **Basic Concepts**: Threads are fundamental units of execution, and synchronization/volatile keywords are used to handle concurrency issues like race conditions.
		2. **Executor Service**: Provides better control over threads via thread pools.
		3. **Future and Callable**: Useful for asynchronous execution and retrieving results.
		4. **Synchronization and Locks**: These are key techniques for managing concurrent access to shared resources.
		5. **Thread Pooling**: Reduces the overhead of thread creation by reusing existing threads.

		These concepts are essential for building robust, scalable multithreaded applications.	
		
		
		


	### **Collections**
	===================================================================
	#### **Concurrent Collections**

	In multithreaded environments, where multiple threads access or modify collections, ensuring thread safety becomes crucial. Java provides several **concurrent collections** that handle synchronization internally, eliminating the need for external synchronization mechanisms like `synchronized` blocks.

	1. **ConcurrentHashMap**:  
	   This is a thread-safe variant of `HashMap`. It is designed to handle concurrent read and write operations without locking the entire map, by partitioning the map into multiple segments. Each segment can be locked independently, thus allowing high concurrency.

	   **Example**:  
	   `ConcurrentHashMap` is widely used in scenarios such as caching, where many threads need to concurrently access or update a shared cache.

	   **Usage**:
	   - Highly effective when you need to perform frequent read and write operations on a map concurrently.
	   - It ensures that the map does not get locked completely during operations, improving performance in multi-threaded applications.

	2. **CopyOnWriteArrayList**:  
	   This is another thread-safe collection where each modification (like `add`, `remove`) creates a new copy of the list. It allows safe iteration even when other threads are modifying the list. However, due to the cost of creating copies, it’s not suitable for applications with high write operations.

	   **Example**:  
	   `CopyOnWriteArrayList` is used in scenarios where the list is frequently read but infrequently modified, such as event listener lists.

	3. **BlockingQueue**:  
	   This is an interface that extends `Queue` and adds blocking operations. It supports methods such as `put()`, `take()`, and `offer()`, which block the thread when the queue is full (in case of `put()`) or empty (in case of `take()`).

	   **Example**:  
	   In producer-consumer problems, a `BlockingQueue` can be used to store data that producers add and consumers remove. The `BlockingQueue` ensures that producers wait when the queue is full, and consumers wait when the queue is empty.

	4. **
	/ConcurrentSkipListSet**:  
	   These collections use a skip list data structure to maintain a sorted order of elements. They allow concurrent reads and writes with thread-safe behavior and can be used when you need a thread-safe, ordered collection.

	   **Example**:  
	   `ConcurrentSkipListMap` can be used in scenarios where you need to maintain key-value pairs in sorted order while handling concurrent operations, such as maintaining a live leaderboard in a multiplayer game.

			---
			| Feature                       | `HashMap`                      | `ConcurrentHashMap`           | `Hashtable`                                  |
		| ----------------------------- | ------------------------------ | ----------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
		| **Thread Safe**               | ❌ No                           | ✅ Yes (lock-striping)         | ✅ Yes (synchronized methods)                 |
		| **Performance**               | ✅ Fast (single-threaded)       | ✅ Better than `Hashtable`     | ❌ Slower (heavy locking)                     |
		| **Null Keys/Values**          | ✅ 1 null key, many null values | ❌ No null keys/values         | ❌ No null keys/values                        |
		| **Synchronization Mechanism** | None                           | Fine-grained (bucket-level)   | Entire method-level lock                     |
		| **Legacy?**                   | ❌ Modern                       | ✅ Modern                      | ✅ Legacy (from Java 1.0)                     |
		| **Use Case**                  | Single-threaded, local cache   | High-concurrency environments | Rarely used; replaced by `ConcurrentHashMap` |
		| **Iterator Fail-Fast**        | ✅ Yes                          | ❌ Weakly consistent           | ✅ Yes                                        |

		Threshold = capacity × load factor

		Capacity: Number of buckets in the HashMap (initially 16 by default).

		Load Factor: A measure of how full the map can get before it needs to resize (default is 0.75).

		Threshold: The point at which the map resizes (doubles the capacity).
		
		HashMap<Integer, String> map = new HashMap<>();

		Default capacity: 16

		Default load factor: 0.75

		So, threshold = 16 × 0.75 = 12

		⏩ When the 13th entry is added, resize happens: capacity becomes 32, and the new threshold becomes 24.
		
		
		
	#### **HashMap Improvements in Java 8**
	In Java 8, `HashMap` was enhanced to handle large numbers of collisions more efficiently and introduced several new methods that simplify working with maps.

	1. **Red-Black Tree for Collision Handling**:  
	   In Java 8, if the number of elements in a bucket exceeds a threshold (typically 8), the map converts the bucket to a red-black tree to optimize performance. This conversion improves search time from O(n) to O(log n), where `n` is the number of elements in the bucket.

	   **Example**:  
	   If you're using `HashMap` to store a large number of records, and many hash collisions occur (i.e., multiple entries end up in the same bucket), this feature helps maintain efficient access and reduces the impact of collisions.

	2. **Compute, Merge, and Replace Methods**:  
	   Java 8 introduced several methods to simplify operations on `HashMap`, such as:
	   - `compute()`: Modifies the value for a specific key using the provided remapping function.
	   - `computeIfAbsent()`: Computes the value for a key only if it is absent, ensuring thread safety in cases where the value should be lazily initialized.
	   - `merge()`: Merges the values for a key, using the provided remapping function.

	   **Example**:  
	   ```java
	   map.computeIfAbsent("key1", k -> "default");
	   ```

	---

	#### **Hashtable**

	`Hashtable` is an older collection class that is **synchronized**, making it thread-safe. However, it has several drawbacks, such as:
	- **No `null` keys or values**: Unlike `HashMap`, `Hashtable` does not allow `null` as a key or value.
	- **Slower performance**: `Hashtable` synchronizes on every method, which makes it slower when compared to modern alternatives like `ConcurrentHashMap`.

	**Example**:  
	`Hashtable` might still be useful in legacy systems where thread safety is critical, but modern applications usually prefer `ConcurrentHashMap` for better performance.

	---

	#### ** SQL Indexing**
	========================

	An **index** is a data structure that improves the speed of data retrieval operations on a database table at the cost of additional space and maintenance overhead during write operations. 
	Indexes help in reducing the number of disk reads required to retrieve rows.
	
	Note:
	-----
	You don’t explicitly call the index in the query; it's used automatically when appropriate.
	The database engine decides when to use the index, based on the query structure.
	Use EXPLAIN to check if the index is being utilized.

	1. **Single-Column Index**:  
	   An index is created on a single column to speed up queries that filter on that column.

	   **Example**:
	   ```sql
	   CREATE INDEX idx_name ON employees(name);
	   ```
	   fetch:
	   SELECT * FROM employee WHERE name = 'John';
	   
	   EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM employees WHERE name = 'John'; //To check whether an index is being used for a query, you can use the EXPLAIN command (or similar) to inspect the query execution plan.


	2. **Composite Index**:  
	   An index created on multiple columns helps optimize queries that filter based on a combination of columns.

	   **Example**:
	   ```sql
	   CREATE INDEX idx_name_age ON employees(name, age);
	   ```

	3. **Unique Index**:  
	   Ensures all values in a column (or combination of columns) are unique, helping to enforce integrity.

	   **Example**:
	   ```sql
	   CREATE UNIQUE INDEX idx_email ON users(email);
	   ```
	   
	   Use Cases : 
	   
	 i. **Index**
	- **Use Case**: Searching for records by a **single column** frequently.
	- **Example**:  
	  - Query: Find users by **email**.
	  - **Why**: Indexing the email column speeds up search queries on email addresses.
	  - **Scenario**: User login, user search by email, etc.

	 ii. **Reducing Lookup Time in Join Operations**
	- **Use Case**: Speeding up **join operations** between multiple tables.
	- **Example**:  
	  - Query: Retrieve orders and customer details by joining `orders` and `customers` tables.
	  - **Why**: Indexing `customer_id` on both tables improves join performance.
	  - **Scenario**: Fetching order details along with customer information.

	 iii. **Optimizing Data Retrieval Based on Frequently Filtered Columns**
	- **Use Case**: When you frequently filter by a **specific column** (e.g., status, flag).
	- **Example**:  
		 - Query: Find all **active** users (`active = true`).
		 - **Why**: Indexing the `active` column speeds up searches for active users.
		 - **Scenario**: Fetching active/inactive users for user management.



	---

	#### **ACID Properties**
	==============================

	ACID stands for **Atomicity**, **Consistency**, **Isolation**, and **Durability**, which are the four key properties that guarantee reliable processing of database transactions.

	1. **Atomicity**:  
		Atomicity means a transaction is treated as a single unit: either everything in it happens, or nothing at all. 
		If any part of the process fails, the whole transaction is cancelled, and the database remains unchanged.
		
		Example: You pay ₹1,000, but if the order placement fails, the ₹1,000 is returned — nothing is half-done.


	2. **Consistency**:  
		Consistency ensures that the data remains valid and follows all rules before and after a transaction. If a transaction would cause invalid data (like breaking a balance limit), it is rejected.
		
		Example: You have ₹500 in your account and try to pay ₹1,000 — the transaction fails because it would break the balance rule
		
	3. **Isolation**:  
		Isolation means that each transaction runs as if it’s the only one happening, even when many users are using the system at the same time. This prevents conflicts or mix-ups.

		Example: You and another user try to buy the last product — only one of you will succeed based on who finishes first.


	4. **Durability**:  
		Durability ensures that once a transaction is successfully completed, the changes are saved permanently, even if the system crashes immediately afterward.

		Example: You get a confirmation message, and even if the website goes down, your order and payment are still recorded.


	---

	#### **Types of Views**
	========================
		A view is a virtual table created using a SQL SELECT query. It does not store data physically but provides a way to simplify queries, restrict data access, and encapsulate complex logic.

	1. **Simple View**:  
		A simple view is created from a single table. It contains no joins, no group functions, and allows some updates if based on key columns.


	   Example:
	   ```sql
	   CREATE VIEW simple_view AS
	   SELECT id, name FROM employees WHERE age > 30;
	   ```

		fetch:
		SELECT * FROM simple_view;
		
	2. **Complex View**:  
		A complex view pulls data from multiple tables using joins, group functions, or subqueries. It is mostly read-only and not directly updatable.

	   **Example**:
	   ```sql
	   CREATE VIEW complex_view AS
	   SELECT e.name, d.department_name
	   FROM employees e
	   JOIN departments d ON e.department_id = d.id;
	   ```
	   
		fetch:
		SELECT * FROM complex_view;

	3. **Updatable View**:  
		If a view is based on a single table and doesn't use joins, aggregations, or group functions, it can usually be updated.

		Example:
		step:1
		------
		CREATE VIEW updatable_view AS
		SELECT id, name, salary FROM employees WHERE department = 'IT';
		
		step:2
		------
		UPDATE updatable_view SET salary = salary + 1000 WHERE id = 101;

		fetch:
		SELECT * FROM updatable_view;

	4. **Non-Updatable View**:  
		Views that involve multiple tables, joins, aggregations (like SUM, AVG), or DISTINCT are typically read-only and not updatable.

		Example:
		CREATE VIEW salary_summary_view AS
		SELECT department_id, AVG(salary) AS avg_salary
		FROM employees
		GROUP BY department_id;

		fetch:
		SELECT * FROM salary_summary_view;

	---

	Here’s a simplified and complete explanation of the **Types of Keys** in SQL, with proper structure, real use cases, and examples.

	---

	### Types of Keys
	-----------------------

	Keys are used in relational databases to uniquely identify rows, enforce integrity, and establish relationships between tables.

	---

	### 1. Primary Key  
	A primary key uniquely identifies each record in a table. It **cannot be null** and **must be unique**. A table can have **only one primary key** (can be a single column or composite).

	**Example:**
	```sql
	CREATE TABLE employees (
		id INT PRIMARY KEY,
		name VARCHAR(100)
	);
	```

	**Real-world use**:  
	Employee ID is unique for each employee, used to identify records accurately.

	---

	### 2. Foreign Key  
	A foreign key is a column (or group of columns) in one table that refers to the **primary key** in another table. It is used to create **relationships between tables**.

	**Example:**
	```sql
	CREATE TABLE departments (
		dept_id INT PRIMARY KEY,
		dept_name VARCHAR(100)
	);

	CREATE TABLE employees (
		id INT PRIMARY KEY,
		name VARCHAR(100),
		dept_id INT,
		FOREIGN KEY (dept_id) REFERENCES departments(dept_id)
	);
	```

	**Real-world use**:  
	An employee belongs to a department. The department ID links employees to the right department.

	---

	### 3. Unique Key  
	A unique key ensures all values in a column (or group of columns) are **distinct**, but **unlike primary key**, it **can have null values**.

	**Example:**
	```sql
	CREATE TABLE users (
		user_id INT PRIMARY KEY,
		email VARCHAR(100) UNIQUE
	);
	```

	**Real-world use**:  
	Email addresses must be unique for login, but a user may choose not to provide it, so it allows null.

	---

	### 4. Composite Key  
	A composite key is a **primary key made of two or more columns**. It uniquely identifies a record using the combination.

	**Example:**
	```sql
	CREATE TABLE course_enrollments (
		student_id INT,
		course_id INT,
		enrollment_date DATE,
		PRIMARY KEY (student_id, course_id)
	);
	```

	**Real-world use**:  
	A student can enroll in multiple courses. The combination of `student_id` and `course_id` ensures uniqueness in enrollments.


	---

	### **Testing**
	====================
	#### **JUnit 5 Basics**

	JUnit 5 is a widely used testing framework for Java that is composed of three sub-projects: **JUnit Platform**, **JUnit Jupiter**, and **JUnit Vintage**. It provides several annotations to define and manage tests.

	1. **@Test**: Marks a method as a test case to be run by JUnit.

	2. **@BeforeEach/@AfterEach**: Run particular method before/after each test method.

	3. **@BeforeAll/@AfterAll**: Run once before/after all test methods in the test class.

	**Example**:
	```java
	@Test
	public void testAddition() {
		assertEquals(4, 2 + 2);
	}
	```

	#### **Unit vs Integration Tests**

	- **Unit Test**: Focuses on testing a single unit (such as a method or class) in isolation. Dependencies are usually mocked using frameworks like Mockito.
	  
	- **Integration Test**: Tests how different units or components work together. It may involve database, APIs, and other services.
	
	
	| Annotation          | Used in Unit Test | Used in Integration Test | Purpose                                                                 |
	|---------------------|-------------------|----------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------|
	| `@Mock`             | ✅ Yes            | 🚫 Not recommended         | Creates a fake object that you control manually (no real logic or DB). |
	| `@InjectMocks`      | ✅ Yes            | 🚫 Not recommended         | Injects the mocks into your class being tested.                        |
	| `@SpringBootTest`   | 🚫 Optional       | ✅ Yes                     | Loads the full Spring context, suitable for integration testing.       |
	| `@AutoConfigureMockMvc` | 🚫 Optional    | ✅ Yes                     | Enables testing with real HTTP requests/responses.                     |


	1. **Unit Test** using `@Mock` and `@InjectMocks`  
	2. **Integration Test** using `@SpringBootTest`, `@AutoConfigureMockMvc`, and real components

	---

	## 🌟 Scenario: Basic `EmployeeController` with Service & Repository

	Let's say we have the following setup:

	### **Employee.java**
	```java
	@Entity
	public class Employee {
		@Id
		@GeneratedValue
		private Long id;
		private String name;
		private String department;
		// getters & setters
	}
	```

	### **EmployeeRepository.java**
	```java
	public interface EmployeeRepository extends JpaRepository<Employee, Long> {
	}
	```

	### **EmployeeService.java**
	```java
	@Service
	public class EmployeeService {
		@Autowired
		private EmployeeRepository repository;

		public Employee save(Employee emp) {
			return repository.save(emp);
		}

		public List<Employee> getAll() {
			return repository.findAll();
		}
	}
	```

	### **EmployeeController.java**
	```java
	@RestController
	@RequestMapping("/employees")
	public class EmployeeController {

		@Autowired
		private EmployeeService service;

		@PostMapping
		public ResponseEntity<Employee> create(@RequestBody Employee emp) {
			return new ResponseEntity<>(service.save(emp), HttpStatus.CREATED);
		}

		@GetMapping
		public List<Employee> getAll() {
			return service.getAll();
		}
	}
	```

	---

	## ✅ 1. Unit Test (using `@Mock` and `@InjectMocks`)

	**EmployeeServiceTest.java**
	```java
	@ExtendWith(MockitoExtension.class)
	public class EmployeeServiceTest {

		@Mock
		private EmployeeRepository repository;

		@InjectMocks
		private EmployeeService service;

		@Test
		void testSaveEmployee() {
			Employee emp = new Employee();
			emp.setName("John");

			when(repository.save(any(Employee.class))).thenReturn(emp);

			Employee saved = service.save(emp);
			assertEquals("John", saved.getName());
		}
	}
	```

	➡️ **Only tests the service**, not the database, not controller.

	---

	## ✅ 2. Integration Test (with full Spring Boot context)

	**EmployeeControllerIntegrationTest.java**
	```java
	@SpringBootTest
	@AutoConfigureMockMvc
	@TestMethodOrder(MethodOrderer.OrderAnnotation.class)
	public class EmployeeControllerIntegrationTest {

		@Autowired
		private MockMvc mockMvc;

		@Autowired
		private ObjectMapper mapper;

		private static Long createdEmployeeId;

		@Test
		@Order(1)
		void testCreateEmployee() throws Exception {
			Employee emp = new Employee();
			emp.setName("Alice");
			emp.setDepartment("HR");

			MvcResult result = mockMvc.perform(post("/employees")
					.contentType(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
					.content(mapper.writeValueAsString(emp)))
					.andExpect(status().isCreated())
					.andExpect(jsonPath("$.name").value("Alice"))
					.andReturn();

			String response = result.getResponse().getContentAsString();
			Employee created = mapper.readValue(response, Employee.class);
			createdEmployeeId = created.getId(); // Save for later use
		}

		@Test
		@Order(2)
		void testGetAllEmployees() throws Exception {
			mockMvc.perform(get("/employees"))
					.andExpect(status().isOk())
					.andExpect(jsonPath("$.length()").value(greaterThanOrEqualTo(1)));
		}

		@Test
		@Order(3)
		void testGetEmployeeById() throws Exception {
			mockMvc.perform(get("/employees/" + createdEmployeeId))
					.andExpect(status().isOk())
					.andExpect(jsonPath("$.name").value("Alice"));
		}

		@Test
		@Order(4)
		void testUpdateEmployee() throws Exception {
			Employee updated = new Employee();
			updated.setName("Alice Updated");
			updated.setDepartment("Finance");

			mockMvc.perform(put("/employees/" + createdEmployeeId)
					.contentType(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
					.content(mapper.writeValueAsString(updated)))
					.andExpect(status().isOk())
					.andExpect(jsonPath("$.department").value("Finance"));
		}

		@Test
		@Order(5)
		void testDeleteEmployee() throws Exception {
			mockMvc.perform(delete("/employees/" + createdEmployeeId))
					.andExpect(status().isNoContent());
		}

		@Test
		@Order(6)
		void testGetAfterDelete() throws Exception {
			mockMvc.perform(get("/employees/" + createdEmployeeId))
					.andExpect(status().isNotFound());
		}
	}

	```

	➡️ **Tests the whole flow**: controller → service → repository → in-memory DB (like H2)  

	---

	## 🔁 Summary

	| Test Type       | Tech Used                      | What It Tests                      | Speed     | Example File                        |
	|-----------------|--------------------------------|------------------------------------|-----------|--------------------------------------|
	| Unit Test       | `@Mock`, `@InjectMocks`        | One class only (e.g., service)     | ⚡ Fast    | `EmployeeServiceTest.java`          |
	| Integration Test| `@SpringBootTest`, real beans  | Full app flow (controller to DB)   | 🐢 Slower | `EmployeeControllerIntegrationTest` |

	#### **Test Containers (Basic)**
	========================================

	Testcontainers is a Java library used for running Docker containers in your tests. It is useful for integration testing where you need services like databases or message brokers.
	You are testing versions (correct data or correct updates) and connections (to databases, APIs, etc.) in your tests.
	You can use Testcontainers to check real database connections and operations in repository tests.


	<dependency>
		<groupId>org.testcontainers</groupId>
		<artifactId>testcontainers</artifactId>
		<version>1.17.6</version> <!-- Use the latest version -->
		<scope>test</scope>
	</dependency>

	<dependency>
		<groupId>org.testcontainers</groupId>
		<artifactId>mysql</artifactId>
		<version>1.17.6</version> <!-- Use the latest version -->
		<scope>test</scope>
	</dependency>

	import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
	import org.junit.jupiter.api.container.Container;
	import org.springframework.beans.factory.annotation.Autowired;
	import org.springframework.boot.test.context.SpringBootTest;
	import org.springframework.jdbc.core.JdbcTemplate;
	import org.testcontainers.containers.MySQLContainer;

	import java.sql.Connection;
	import java.sql.DriverManager;
	import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertNotNull;
	import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertTrue;

	@SpringBootTest
	public class MySQLIntegrationTest {

		// Define a MySQL container and initialize it
		@Container
		public MySQLContainer<?> mysql = new MySQLContainer<>("mysql:8.0")
				.withDatabaseName("testdb")
				.withUsername("testuser")
				.withPassword("testpass");

		@Autowired
		private JdbcTemplate jdbcTemplate;

		// Test case to verify MySQL database connection
		@Test
		void testDatabaseConnection() throws Exception {
			try (Connection conn = DriverManager.getConnection(mysql.getJdbcUrl(), mysql.getUsername(), mysql.getPassword())) {
				assertNotNull(conn);  // Ensure the connection is not null, i.e., it's successful
			}
		}

		// Test case for inserting data into the MySQL container and retrieving it
		@Test
		void testDatabaseInsertAndRetrieve() {
			// Insert data into the MySQL container
			jdbcTemplate.update("CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS employees (id INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY, name VARCHAR(255), department VARCHAR(255))");
			jdbcTemplate.update("INSERT INTO employees (name, department) VALUES ('Alice', 'HR')");

			// Retrieve the data to verify it was inserted
			Integer count = jdbcTemplate.queryForObject("SELECT COUNT(*) FROM employees WHERE name = 'Alice'", Integer.class);
			
			assertNotNull(count);
			assertTrue(count > 0);  // Assert that data is inserted
		}
	}
