	ANSWERS FOR ABOVE WEAK AREA - 2
	==============================================

		1. Advanced Angular JS Concepts
		---------------------------------
		 1.1 HostBinding and HostListener

		HostBinding: Used to set or change a property or attribute of the HTML element where the directive/component is used
		HostListener: Used to listen for events like click, mouseover on that element
		Example:

		```ts
		@HostBinding('class.active') isActive = true; // adds class 'active'
		@HostListener('click') onClick() {
		  console.log('Element clicked!');
		}
		```

		 1.2 RXJS and Observables

		Observables: are used to handle data that arrives over time, such as API responses, user inputs, or even events. They are a key part of working with asynchronous data streams in Angular.
		
		RXJS (Reactive Extensions for JavaScript): RXJS is a library that provides a set of operators to work with Observables in a more powerful and declarative way.
		
		Key Concepts:
		Observable: Represents a stream of data that arrives over time.
		Subscriber: The function that gets called when data arrives in the Observable.
		Operator: Functions that allow you to transform, filter, or combine data streams.

		Example:
		```ts
		this.http.get('/api/data').subscribe(data => console.log(data)); // API call and response
		```

		 1.3 Custom Pipes

		Pipes: Used to format or transform data in HTML (like uppercase, date, currency)
		Custom Pipe: You can create your own logic to format data
		Pure Pipe: Called only when input changes
		Impure Pipe: Called very often, even when input may not change
		Example:

		```ts
		@Pipe({ name: 'uppercasePipe' })
		export class UppercasePipe implements PipeTransform {
		  transform(value: string): string {
			return value.toUpperCase();
		  }
		}
		```

		 1.4 Angular Directives

		Directives: Used to change the structure or style of the DOM
		Structural Directives: Change layout (like \*ngIf, \*ngFor)
		Attribute Directives: Change appearance or behavior (like ngStyle, ngClass)

		 1.5 Dependency Injection in Angular

		Dependency Injection: A design pattern where Angular provides the required objects like services automatically
		It reduces tight coupling and makes the code testable
		Example:

		```ts
		constructor(private userService: UserService) {} // service is injected here
		```

		---

		## 2. Java Core Concepts

		 2.1 volatile Keyword

		Ensures that when one thread changes a variable, the new value is visible to other threads
		Used in multi-threaded programs to prevent outdated values

		 2.2 Atomic Classes

		Provide thread-safe operations without using locks
		Example: AtomicInteger, AtomicBoolean
		Used to avoid race conditions

		 2.3.1 Java Reflection
					Java Reflection is a powerful API that allows you to:
					 - Inspect classes, interfaces, methods, fields, and constructors at runtime.
					 - Instantiate objects.
					 - Invoke methods or access fields dynamically (without knowing class names at compile time)

					Example:

					```java
						import java.lang.reflect.*;
						package com.example;

						public class MyClass {
							private String message = "Hello from Reflection!";

							public MyClass() {}

							public MyClass(String msg) {
								this.message = msg;
							}

							public void showMessage() {
								System.out.println("Message: " + message);
							}

							private void secret() {
								System.out.println("This is a private method.");
							}
						}

						public class ReflectionExample {
							public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {

								// Load class
								Class<?> clazz = Class.forName("com.example.MyClass");

								// Access and call default constructor
								Object obj = clazz.getDeclaredConstructor().newInstance();

								// Access and modify private field
								Field field = clazz.getDeclaredField("message");
								field.setAccessible(true); // Make private field accessible
								field.set(obj, "Modified via Reflection!");

								// Call public method
								Method showMessage = clazz.getMethod("showMessage");
								showMessage.invoke(obj);  // Output: Message: Modified via Reflection!

								// Call private method
								Method secretMethod = clazz.getDeclaredMethod("secret");
								secretMethod.setAccessible(true);
								secretMethod.invoke(obj); // Output: This is a private method.
							}
						}
						
					```
					
					2.3.2 Java Generics

					What is Generics?

					- Generics allow you to write code that works with any data type without losing type safety.

					Why Use Generics?
					
					Type Safety: Catch type errors at compile time.
					Code Reusability: Write generic methods and classes that work for many types.
					Eliminate Type Casting: No need to cast objects when using generics.
					
					Package: java.lang.reflect
					
					---

					1. Generic Class Example

					```java
					class Box<T> {
						private T value;

						public void set(T value) {
							this.value = value;
						}

						public T get() {
							return value;
						}
					}
					```

					Usage:

					```java
					Box<String> stringBox = new Box<>();
					stringBox.set("Hello");
					System.out.println(stringBox.get());  // Output: Hello
					```

					Here, `T` is replaced by `String`.

					---

					2. Generic Method Example

					```java
					public class Utility {
						public static <T> void printArray(T[] array) {
							for (T item : array) {
								System.out.println(item);
							}
						}
					}
					```

					Usage:

					```java
					Integer[] numbers = {1, 2, 3};
					Utility.printArray(numbers);  // Works with any type of array
					```

					---

					3. Bounded Generics

					Use bounds to restrict types that can be passed.

					```java
					class Data<T extends Number> {
						T value;
					}
					```

					* Now `T` can only be a `Number` or its subclass (`Integer`, `Double`, etc.)

					---

					4. Wildcards (`?`)

					* `?` is an unknown type.
					* Used for flexibility when reading data.

					```java
					public void printList(List<?> list) {
						for (Object obj : list) {
							System.out.println(obj);
						}
					}
					```

					* You can read items but can’t add to the list (except null).

					---

					5. Generic Interface Example

					```java
					interface Storage<T> {
						void add(T item);
						T get(int index);
					}
					```

					Implementation:

					```java
					class StringStorage implements Storage<String> {
						private List<String> list = new ArrayList<>();

						public void add(String item) {
							list.add(item);
						}

						public String get(int index) {
							return list.get(index);
						}
					}
					```
					Summary

					| Concept            | Meaning                                      |
					| ------------------ | -------------------------------------------- |
					| `<T>`              | Generic type placeholder                     |
					| `<?>`              | Unknown wildcard type                        |
					| `<T extends Type>` | Bounded type (only subclass of Type allowed) |
					| Reusability        | Use same code for multiple data types        |
					| Type safety        | Avoid `ClassCastException` at runtime        |


		 2.4 DRY Principle

		DRY: Don't Repeat Yourself
		Focus on writing reusable and modular code
		Avoid duplicating the same logic in multiple places
		
		like convering DTO into Entity and Entity into DTO
		
		---

		## 3. Spring Core

		 3.1 Bean Scopes

		singleton: Only one object is created (default scope)
		prototype: A new object is created every time it's requested
		request: One bean per HTTP request (only for web apps)
		session: One bean per HTTP session

		 3.2 ApplicationContext vs BeanFactory

		BeanFactory: Basic container, loads beans lazily
		ApplicationContext: More features like internationalization, event handling, AOP, loads beans eagerly

		 3.3 @PostConstruct and @PreDestroy

		@PostConstruct: Runs a method after the bean is created
		@PreDestroy: Runs a method before the bean is destroyed
		Used for initialization and cleanup

		---

		## 4. Spring Data JPA (Advanced)

		@Query: Used to write custom queries in a repository. Use Criteria API for dynamic queries
		Criteria API: Allows building dynamic and type-safe queries in Java
		Example:

		```java
		@Query("SELECT p FROM Product p WHERE p.price BETWEEN :min AND :max")
		List<Product> findByPriceRange(@Param("min") int min, @Param("max") int max);
		```

		---

		## 5. Spring REST APIs

		 5.1 ResponseEntity

		Represents the full HTTP response, including body, status code, and headers
		Example:

		```java
		return ResponseEntity.ok(product);
				| Feature     | Benefit                      |
		| ----------- | ---------------------------- |
		| `body()`    | Returns a response body      |
		| `status()`  | Sets HTTP status code        |
		| `headers()` | Allows custom headers        |
		| `build()`   | Builds response without body |
		
		
		✅ Examples of Headers You Can Add:
		
		| Header Name       | Purpose                                                                 |
		| ----------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------- |
		| `Content-Type`    | Specifies the MIME type of the response (e.g., `application/json`)      |
		| `Location`        | Used in `201 Created` responses to indicate the URL of the new resource |
		| `Cache-Control`   | Controls caching behavior of clients                                    |
		| `Set-Cookie`      | Used to set cookies                                                     |
		| `Authorization`   | Used for tokens or credentials                                          |
		| `X-Custom-Header` | Custom header for internal use                                          |

		```

		 5.2 Content Negotiation

		Used to return different response formats like JSON or XML based on `Accept` header
		Example:
		`Accept: application/json` or `Accept: application/xml`

		 5.3 PATCH vs PUT

		PUT: Replaces the whole object
		PATCH: Updates only the given fields of the object

		---

		## 6. Design Patterns

		 6.1 Structural Patterns

		Used to simplify relationships between objects
		Examples: Adapter (convert interface), Decorator (add features dynamically)

		 6.2 Behavioral Patterns

		Used to define communication between objects
		Examples: Observer (notify changes), Strategy (select algorithm at runtime)

		 6.3 Factory and Builder

		Factory Pattern: Creates objects without exposing creation logic
		Builder Pattern: Used to build complex objects step by step

		---

		## 7. Microservices Design

		 7.1 CQRS Pattern

		CQRS: Command Query Responsibility Segregation
		Separates read (queries) and write (commands) logic for scalability and clarity

		 7.2 Circuit Breaker

		Stops calling a failing service to prevent overload
		Used to make the system more stable
		Example: Netflix Hystrix

		 7.3 API Gateway

		A single entry point to access multiple microservices
		Handles authentication, routing, rate limiting

		 7.4 Service Discovery

		Helps services to find and communicate with each other
		Example: Eureka, Consul

		 7.5 Communication

		Synchronous: Uses HTTP/REST, real-time communication
		Asynchronous: Uses messaging like Kafka, RabbitMQ for better performance

		---

		## 8. Kafka and Messaging Queues

		 8.1 Kafka Basics

		Producer: Sends data
		Consumer: Reads data
		Topic: Category or channel where data is published
		Partition: Divides data for better performance

		 8.2 Dead Letter Queue (DLQ)

		Stores failed or problematic messages
		Used to debug or retry failed events later

		 8.3 Fault Tolerance

		Kafka stores multiple copies of data in different brokers
		If one fails, another takes over

		---

		## 9. NoSQL and CAP Theorem

		 9.1 NoSQL Types

		Document: Stores data in JSON-like format (MongoDB)
		Key-Value: Simple pair storage (Redis)
		Column: Data in columns instead of rows (Cassandra)
		Graph: Stores relationships between entities (Neo4j)

		 9.2 CAP Theorem

		A distributed system can have only two of the three:
		Consistency: All nodes have the same data
		Availability: System responds even if part is down
		Partition Tolerance: Works even when communication between nodes is broken

		---

		## 10. CI/CD and Software Engineering

		 10.1 SonarQube and Checkmarx

		SonarQube: Finds bugs, code smells, maintains code quality
		Checkmarx: Checks code for security issues

		 10.2 Scaling

		Vertical Scaling: Add more power (CPU, RAM) to a single server
		Horizontal Scaling: Add more servers to share the load

		 10.3 CI/CD Pipeline

		CI (Continuous Integration): Automatically tests and integrates code
		CD (Continuous Deployment): Automatically deploys code to production after tests pass

		---



How to improve application performance 
======================================
Sure! Here's your complete content in **plain text** format with proper headings and code blocks—no bolds or styles, just clean and readable for conversion into a text file, Word doc, or PDF.

---

# How to Improve Application Performance in Spring Boot

1. Using Cache in Spring Boot
2. Using In-Memory Database in Spring Boot
3. Using Spring Batch for Bulk Processing
4. Using Pagination in Spring Boot

---

1. Using Cache in Spring Boot

---

Theory:
Caching improves performance by storing frequently accessed data in memory, avoiding repetitive DB queries.

Technologies:

* @EnableCaching
* @Cacheable, @CachePut, @CacheEvict
* Cache Providers: EhCache, Caffeine, Redis, ConcurrentMap (default)

Example (Employee Management):

```java
// Main Class
@SpringBootApplication
@EnableCaching
public class EmployeeApp {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        SpringApplication.run(EmployeeApp.class, args);
    }
}

// EmployeeService.java
@Service
public class EmployeeService {

    @Autowired
    private EmployeeRepository employeeRepository;

    @Cacheable("employees")
    public Employee getEmployeeById(Long id) {
        System.out.println("Fetching from DB...");
        return employeeRepository.findById(id).orElseThrow();
    }
	
	@CacheEvict(value = "employees", allEntries = true)
    public void clearAllCache() {
        System.out.println("All employee cache cleared");
    }
}

// EmployeeController.java
@RestController
public class EmployeeController {

    @Autowired
    private EmployeeService employeeService;

    @GetMapping("/employees/{id}")
    public Employee getEmployee(@PathVariable Long id) {
        return employeeService.getEmployeeById(id);
    }
	
	@DeleteMapping("/cache/clear")
    public String clearCache() {
        employeeService.clearAllCache();
        return "All employee cache cleared.";
    }
}
```

Output:

* First call fetches from DB.
* Subsequent calls use cache (faster, DB not hit).

---

✅ 2. Using In-Memory Data Structure in Spring Boot

---

📘 Theory:

Sometimes we want to **store data temporarily in memory** without using a database. This is useful when:

* You want to **store and access data very fast**
* You're building **batch jobs** and don't want to hit the database every time
* You're creating **temporary queues or caches**

In such cases, we can use **Java Collections** (like `Queue`, `Map`, `List`) in memory, inside a Spring-managed component.

---

💡 Benefits:

* Super fast (no DB access)
* Good for **temporary storage**, batch processing, or testing
* Thread-safe when using `ConcurrentLinkedQueue`

---
✅ Example: Store Employees in Memory using a Queue

#### 🔸 Step 1: Create the `InMemoryEmployeeStore`

```java
package com.company.configuration.in_memory;

import com.company.entity.Employee;
import org.springframework.stereotype.Component;

import java.util.Queue;
import java.util.concurrent.ConcurrentLinkedQueue;

@Component
public class InMemoryEmployeeStore {

    private final Queue<Employee> employeeQueue = new ConcurrentLinkedQueue<>();

    public void add(Employee employee) {
        employeeQueue.add(employee);
    }

    public void addAll(Iterable<Employee> employees) {
        employees.forEach(employeeQueue::add);
    }

    public Queue<Employee> getEmployeeQueue() {
        return employeeQueue;
    }

    public void clear() {
        employeeQueue.clear();
    }
}
```

---
🔸 Step 2: Write Employees to Store using Spring Batch

```java
package com.company.configuration.in_memory;

import com.company.entity.Employee;
import org.springframework.batch.item.Chunk;
import org.springframework.batch.item.ItemWriter;
import org.springframework.stereotype.Component;

@Component
public class InMemoryEmployeeWriter implements ItemWriter<Employee> {

    private final InMemoryEmployeeStore store;

    public InMemoryEmployeeWriter(InMemoryEmployeeStore store) {
        this.store = store;
    }

    @Override
    public void write(Chunk<? extends Employee> chunk) {
        chunk.getItems().forEach(store::add);
    }
}
```

---

📥 Usage in Controller (Optional)

You can expose the in-memory data through an API:

```java
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/employees")
public class EmployeeController {

    private final InMemoryEmployeeStore store;

    public EmployeeController(InMemoryEmployeeStore store) {
        this.store = store;
    }

    @GetMapping("/in-memory")
    public List<Employee> getAllInMemoryEmployees() {
        return new ArrayList<>(store.getEmployeeQueue());
    }
}
```

---

📌 Summary Table

| Feature       | Description                                   |
| ------------- | --------------------------------------------- |
| Store Type    | `ConcurrentLinkedQueue<Employee>` (in memory) |
| Where Stored  | In a Spring `@Component`                      |
| Use Case      | Temporary fast storage during processing      |
| Bonus Feature | Can be used with Spring Batch writers         |

---

3. Using Spring Batch for Bulk Processing

---

Theory:
Spring Batch is ideal for large volume processing (e.g., importing/exporting thousands of employees).

Use Cases:

* Scheduled imports
* Payroll generation
* Report generation

Components:

* ItemReader (reads from CSV/DB)
* ItemProcessor (transforms data)
* ItemWriter (writes to DB)

CSV Example - employees.csv:

```
1,John,IT
2,Sarah,HR
```

Step Configuration:

```java
@Bean
public FlatFileItemReader<Employee> reader() {
    FlatFileItemReader<Employee> reader = new FlatFileItemReader<>();
    reader.setResource(new ClassPathResource("employees.csv"));
    reader.setLineMapper(new DefaultLineMapper<>() {{
        setLineTokenizer(new DelimitedLineTokenizer() {{
            setNames("id", "name", "department");
        }});
        setFieldSetMapper(new BeanWrapperFieldSetMapper<>() {{
            setTargetType(Employee.class);
        }});
    }});
    return reader;
}

@Bean
public EmployeeItemProcessor processor() {
    return new EmployeeItemProcessor();
}

@Bean
public JdbcBatchItemWriter<Employee> writer(DataSource dataSource) {
    JdbcBatchItemWriter<Employee> writer = new JdbcBatchItemWriter<>();
    writer.setSql("INSERT INTO employee (id, name, department) VALUES (:id, :name, :department)");
    writer.setItemSqlParameterSourceProvider(new BeanPropertyItemSqlParameterSourceProvider<>());
    writer.setDataSource(dataSource);
    return writer;
}

@Bean
public Step step() {
    return stepBuilderFactory.get("step")
            .<Employee, Employee>chunk(10)
            .reader(reader())
            .processor(processor())
            .writer(writer(dataSource))
            .build();
}
```

---

4. Using Pagination in Spring Boot

---

Theory:
Pagination avoids fetching all records at once, reducing memory load and DB time.

Spring Data JPA:
Supports pagination using Pageable and Page<T>.

Repository:

```java
public interface EmployeeRepository extends JpaRepository<Employee, Long> {
    Page<Employee> findByDepartment(String dept, Pageable pageable);
}
```

Service:

```java
@Service
public class EmployeeService {

    @Autowired
    private EmployeeRepository employeeRepository;

    public Page<Employee> getEmployeesByDept(String dept, int page, int size) {
        Pageable pageable = PageRequest.of(page, size);
        return employeeRepository.findByDepartment(dept, pageable);
    }
}
```

Controller:

```java
@RestController
public class EmployeeController {

    @Autowired
    private EmployeeService employeeService;

    @GetMapping("/employees")
    public Page<Employee> getEmployees(
            @RequestParam String dept,
            @RequestParam int page,
            @RequestParam int size) {
        return employeeService.getEmployeesByDept(dept, page, size);
    }
}
```

---

## Summary Table

| Technique    | Improves                 | Example Use Case                |
| ------------ | ------------------------ | ------------------------------- |
| Caching      | Response speed           | Get employee by ID              |
| In-Memory DB | Dev/test performance     | H2 for prototyping              |
| Spring Batch | Large data processing    | Bulk import of employee records |
| Pagination   | Efficient data retrieval | Employee list page in UI        |

			AWS CLOUD Services
			====================
			springboot application microservice
			A Spring Boot microservice is a small, independent application that performs a specific business function. It typically includes:

			* Spring Boot starter dependencies
			* A controller layer to expose REST APIs
			* A service layer for business logic
			* A repository layer to interact with the database

			It is usually packaged as a jar file and can be run independently.

			gitlab file
			A `.gitlab-ci.yml` file is used in GitLab to define the CI/CD pipeline. It contains stages, jobs, and scripts to automate building, testing, and deploying applications.

			Example:

			```
			stages:
			  - build
			  - test
			  - deploy

			build-job:
			  stage: build
			  script:
				- ./mvnw clean package -DskipTests

			test-job:
			  stage: test
			  script:
				- ./mvnw test

			deploy-job:
			  stage: deploy
			  script:
				- echo "Deploying application"
			```

			deployement.yaml
			A Kubernetes deployment file is used to manage the deployment of a containerized application in a Kubernetes cluster.

			```
			apiVersion: apps/v1
			kind: Deployment
			metadata:
			  name: my-service
			spec:
			  replicas: 2
			  selector:
				matchLabels:
				  app: my-service
			  template:
				metadata:
				  labels:
					app: my-service
				spec:
				  containers:
				  - name: my-service
					image: <aws_account_id>.dkr.ecr.<region>.amazonaws.com/my-service:latest
					ports:
					- containerPort: 8080
			```

			service.yaml
			A Kubernetes service file exposes the deployment to the network.

			```
			apiVersion: v1
			kind: Service
			metadata:
			  name: my-service
			spec:
			  type: LoadBalancer
			  selector:
				app: my-service
			  ports:
				- protocol: TCP
				  port: 80
				  targetPort: 8080
			```

			docker
			Docker is used to package the Spring Boot microservice as a container image.

			Dockerfile:

			```
			FROM openjdk:17
			COPY target/my-service.jar app.jar
			ENTRYPOINT ["java","-jar","/app.jar"]
			```

			Build and push image:

			```
			docker build -t my-service .
			docker tag my-service:latest <aws_account_id>.dkr.ecr.<region>.amazonaws.com/my-service:latest
			```

			AWS ECR
			Elastic Container Registry is a fully managed Docker container registry that makes it easy to store, manage, and deploy Docker container images.

			Steps:

			* Authenticate Docker to ECR
			* Create a repository
			* Push the Docker image to ECR

			```
			aws ecr get-login-password --region <region> | docker login --username AWS --password-stdin <aws_account_id>.dkr.ecr.<region>.amazonaws.com
			aws ecr create-repository --repository-name my-service
			docker push <aws_account_id>.dkr.ecr.<region>.amazonaws.com/my-service:latest
			```

			AWS EKS
			Elastic Kubernetes Service is a managed Kubernetes service that allows you to run Kubernetes workloads on AWS.

			Steps:

			* Create EKS cluster
			* Configure `kubectl` using AWS CLI
			* Deploy application using `deployment.yaml` and `service.yaml`

			```
			aws eks update-kubeconfig --name my-cluster
			kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml
			kubectl apply -f service.yaml
			```

			AWS EC2
			Elastic Compute Cloud provides virtual machines to run applications. You can run your Docker container or Spring Boot jar directly on EC2.

			Steps:

			* Launch an EC2 instance
			* Install Docker or Java
			* Copy the image or jar
			* Run the application

			```
			scp target/my-service.jar ec2-user@<ec2-ip>:/home/ec2-user
			ssh ec2-user@<ec2-ip>
			java -jar my-service.jar
			```

			AWS Lambda
			Lambda is a serverless compute service. Spring Boot applications can be converted into Lambda functions using AWS Serverless Java Container.

			Steps:

			* Convert Spring Boot app to handler-based Lambda
			* Use AWS SAM CLI or deploy via AWS Console
			* Triggers like API Gateway can invoke the function

			Handler class example:

			```
			public class StreamLambdaHandler 
				extends SpringBootRequestHandler<APIGatewayProxyRequestEvent, APIGatewayProxyResponseEvent> {}
			```

			AWS S3
			Simple Storage Service is used to store and retrieve any amount of data. It can be used to store logs, images, configuration files, or even application builds.

			Steps:

			* Create an S3 bucket
			* Upload files using AWS CLI or SDK

			```
			aws s3 mb s3://my-bucket
			aws s3 cp my-service.jar s3://my-bucket/
			```









			Types of deployements
			======================
			1. Jenkins + Tomcat on VDI (Virtual Desktop Infrastructure)
			2. AWS with Docker, ECR, EKS
			3. AWS Lambda (Serverless)

			---

			1. Jenkins + Tomcat on VDI

			Description:

			* Traditional way of deploying applications
			* Jenkins pulls code, builds WAR/JAR, and deploys to Apache Tomcat installed on a Virtual Machine (VDI or EC2)

			Key Points:

			* Manual or scripted deployment process
			* Application runs inside a servlet container (Tomcat)
			* Limited scalability and automation
			* You manage server setup, patches, and scaling
			* Downtime during redeployments
			* Suitable for small, simple, or legacy systems

			Use Case:

			* Small applications or internal tools in stable environments with minimal scaling

			---

			2. AWS with Docker, ECR, EKS

			Description:

			* Modern container-based deployment
			* Spring Boot app is packaged into Docker, pushed to ECR, and deployed to Kubernetes (EKS)

			Key Points:

			* Highly scalable and fault-tolerant
			* Docker provides consistent environments
			* Kubernetes handles orchestration, scaling, and rolling updates
			* Uses infrastructure-as-code with deployment.yaml and service.yaml
			* Easy to manage microservices
			* Suitable for CI/CD and large-scale production

			Use Case:

			* Microservices architecture, high availability systems, modern DevOps pipelines

			---

			3. AWS Lambda (Serverless)

			Description:

			* Serverless deployment with no servers to manage
			* Spring Boot app adapted as a handler and deployed as a Lambda function
			* Triggered by events like HTTP via API Gateway, S3 upload, etc.

			Key Points:

			* Pay only for execution time
			* Automatically scalable and cost-effective for low to medium traffic
			* Cold start issues with Java (initial delay)
			* Limited execution time (15 minutes max)
			* Not suited for full web apps but good for small tasks or services

			Use Case:

			* Event-driven architecture, lightweight services, background jobs, scheduled tasks, APIs with intermittent usage

			---

			Summary Table:

			| Feature          | Jenkins + Tomcat (VDI)   | AWS Docker + ECR + EKS         | AWS Lambda                 |
			| ---------------- | ------------------------ | ------------------------------ | -------------------------- |
			| Setup Complexity | Simple (manual)          | Medium to High (infra as code) | Medium (Lambda packaging)  |
			| Scalability      | Manual                   | Auto via Kubernetes            | Auto by AWS                |
			| Cost             | Fixed (always running)   | Depends on usage and infra     | Pay-per-use                |
			| DevOps Maturity  | Basic CI/CD              | Full CI/CD + Orchestration     | Event-driven CI/CD         |
			| Suitable For     | Legacy apps, small teams | Microservices, production apps | Serverless functions, APIs |
			| Cold Start Time  | No                       | No                             | Yes (Java-based apps)      |
			| Infra Management | Manual                   | Automated (IaC, Kubernetes)    | None                       |
==========================================================================================================================================================================================

	==========================================
	### SOLID Principles
	==========================================

		The SOLID principles are a set of five design principles that help make software designs more understandable, flexible, and maintainable. They were introduced by Robert C. Martin (Uncle Bob). 
		Let's go over each principle with real-world examples to better understand their application.
		
		Solid Priciples :
		----------------
		1. **Single Responsibility Principle (SRP)**
		   A class should do only one specific task or job, so it has only one reason to change.

		2. **Open/Closed Principle (OCP)**
		   Your code should allow new features to be added without changing the existing working code.

		3. **Liskov Substitution Principle (LSP)**
		   Subclasses should be able to replace their parent classes without causing errors or unexpected behavior.

		4. **Interface Segregation Principle (ISP)**
		   Don’t force a class to implement methods it doesn’t use — create smaller, more focused interfaces.

		5. **Dependency Inversion Principle (DIP)**
		   High-level modules should not depend on low-level modules; both should depend on abstract interfaces.

		---

		1. **Single Responsibility Principle (SRP)**
		---------------------------------------------
		
			**Definition:**
			The Single Responsibility Principle states that a class should have only one reason to change, meaning it should focus on only one job or task.

			**Real-World Example:**
			Imagine a **Book** class in a library system. If the **Book** class is responsible for both storing book information (like title, author, etc.) and managing its printing format (e.g., PDF, HTML), it violates SRP. Instead, it would be better to split the responsibilities into two classes:

			- **Book Class**: Handles book details.
			- **BookPrinter Class**: Handles the printing functionality.

			**Why it matters:**  
			By adhering to SRP, each class can evolve independently. Changes to printing logic don’t affect the book’s properties and vice versa.

		---


		2. **Open/Closed Principle (OCP)**
		-----------------------------------
		
			**Definition:**
			Software entities (classes, modules, functions, etc.) should be open for extension but closed for modification.
			simple terms : A class or module should be easily extended to add new features, but you shouldn’t have to change its existing code to do that.

			**Better Explanation:**
			When your software needs new behavior, you should be able to **add it by writing new code**, not by **modifying existing code** that is already working. This makes your code more stable and less likely to break when changes are needed.

			This is usually done using:

			* **Interfaces**
			* **Abstract classes**
			* **Polymorphism**

			**Real-World Example:**
			
			Imagine you have a payment system that processes credit card payments. Later, you want to add PayPal support.
			Instead of modifying the existing `PaymentProcessor` class, you should:

			* Create a `Payment` interface (or abstract class)
			* Have `CreditCardPayment` and `PayPalPayment` implement that interface

			This way, your main code doesn’t change — you just **extend** it with new classes.

			---

			Let me know if you'd like examples in Java or another language.


			## ❌ **Without Following OCP** (Bad Practice)

			Here, we **modify the existing class** every time we add a new payment method.

			```java
			public class PaymentProcessor {
				public void processPayment(String paymentType) {
					if (paymentType.equals("creditcard")) {
						System.out.println("Processing credit card payment");
					} else if (paymentType.equals("paypal")) {
						System.out.println("Processing PayPal payment");
					} else if (paymentType.equals("upi")) {
						System.out.println("Processing UPI payment");
					}
				}
			}
			```

			### 🔴 Problems:

			* If you want to add a new type (e.g., Google Pay), you must **modify the `processPayment()` method**.
			* Over time, this class becomes harder to maintain and test.

			---

			## ✅ **With OCP (Open for Extension, Closed for Modification)**

			Here, we **use an interface**, so we can extend functionality by adding **new classes** instead of changing existing ones.

			### Step 1: Create an interface

			```java
			public interface Payment {
				void pay();
			}
			```

			### Step 2: Create separate classes for each payment type

			```java
			public class CreditCardPayment implements Payment {
				public void pay() {
					System.out.println("Processing credit card payment");
				}
			}

			public class PayPalPayment implements Payment {
				public void pay() {
					System.out.println("Processing PayPal payment");
				}
			}

			public class UPIPayment implements Payment {
				public void pay() {
					System.out.println("Processing UPI payment");
				}
			}
			```

			### Step 3: Use them in a loosely-coupled way

			```java
			public class PaymentProcessor {
				public void processPayment(Payment payment) {
					payment.pay();
				}
			}
			```

			### ✅ Now you can extend easily:

			```java
			public class Main {
				public static void main(String[] args) {
					PaymentProcessor processor = new PaymentProcessor();

					processor.processPayment(new CreditCardPayment());
					processor.processPayment(new PayPalPayment());
					processor.processPayment(new UPIPayment());
				}
			}
			```

			### ✅ Benefits:

			* No need to change `PaymentProcessor` when adding a new payment method.
			* Follows **Open/Closed Principle**: open for extension, closed for modification.

			---


		3. **Liskov Substitution Principle (LSP)**
		-------------------------------------------
		
		**Definition:**
		Objects of a parent class should be able to be replaced with objects of its child class without breaking the functionality of the program.
		
		In other words, if a class B is a subclass of class A, you should be able to use an object of class B wherever class A is expected, and the program should still work correctly.

		### **Without LSP (Problematic Approach)**

		In this example, the `Refund` class is incorrectly extending the `Payment` class, which violates the Liskov Substitution Principle:

		```java
		// Base class for all payments
		public class Payment {
			public void pay() {
				System.out.println("Processing payment...");
			}
		}

		// Credit Card Payment subclass
		public class CreditCardPayment extends Payment {
			public void pay() {
				System.out.println("Processing credit card payment...");
			}
		}

		// Cash Payment subclass
		public class CashPayment extends Payment {
			public void pay() {
				System.out.println("Processing cash payment...");
			}
		}

		// Refund subclass (problematic)
		public class Refund extends Payment {
			@Override
			public void pay() {
				System.out.println("Processing refund..."); // Not a valid payment, it's a refund
			}
		}

		public class Main {
			public static void main(String[] args) {
				Payment payment = new CreditCardPayment();
				payment.pay(); // Expected: "Processing credit card payment..."
				
				payment = new CashPayment();
				payment.pay(); // Expected: "Processing cash payment..."
				
				payment = new Refund();
				payment.pay(); // Problem: "Processing refund..."
			}
		}
		```

		### ❌ **Problem:**

		The `Refund` class, which isn't really a "payment," is wrongly inheriting from the `Payment` class. This can cause confusion in the system, especially if your code assumes that every subclass of `Payment` is related to making payments.

		---

		### **With LSP (Correct Approach)**

		Here’s how to improve the design by following the Liskov Substitution Principle:

		```java
		// Base class for payment actions
		public class PaymentAction {
			public void execute() {
				System.out.println("Executing payment action...");
			}
		}

		// Subclass for Credit Card Payment action
		public class CreditCardPaymentAction extends PaymentAction {
			public void execute() {
				System.out.println("Processing credit card payment...");
			}
		}

		// Subclass for Cash Payment action
		public class CashPaymentAction extends PaymentAction {
			public void execute() {
				System.out.println("Processing cash payment...");
			}
		}

		// Refund class should be a separate action, not a Payment subclass
		public class RefundAction {
			public void execute() {
				System.out.println("Processing refund...");
			}
		}

		public class Main {
			public static void main(String[] args) {
				// Execute payment actions
				PaymentAction paymentAction = new CreditCardPaymentAction();
				paymentAction.execute(); // Expected: "Processing credit card payment..."
				
				paymentAction = new CashPaymentAction();
				paymentAction.execute(); // Expected: "Processing cash payment..."
				
				// Refund should be handled separately
				RefundAction refundAction = new RefundAction();
				refundAction.execute(); // Expected: "Processing refund..."
			}
		}
		```

		### ✅ **Explanation:**

		* The **`RefundAction`** is now a **separate class** because it represents something different from payments.
		* The `PaymentAction` and its subclasses (like `CreditCardPaymentAction` and `CashPaymentAction`) are kept for actions related to payments.
		* This way, we **maintain consistency** and **don’t violate the Liskov Substitution Principle** by ensuring that subclasses properly align with their intended roles.

		---

		This approach keeps the system predictable and easy to maintain!


		4. **Interface Segregation Principle (ISP)**
		--------------------------------------------
		
			**Definition:**
			Clients should not be forced to depend on interfaces they do not use. This principle suggests that larger interfaces should be split into smaller, more specific ones.

			**Real-World Example:**
			Consider an interface for a **Printer** that includes methods for both printing and scanning:

		```java
		public interface MultiFunctionPrinter {
			void print();
			void scan();
			void fax();
		}

		public class LaserPrinter implements MultiFunctionPrinter {
			public void print() {
				// Print document
			}

			public void scan() {
				// Scan document
			}

			public void fax() {
				// Fax document
			}
		}

		public class InkjetPrinter implements MultiFunctionPrinter {
			public void print() {
				// Print document
			}

			public void scan() {
				// This is problematic for an Inkjet printer that doesn't scan.
			}

			public void fax() {
				// This is problematic for an Inkjet printer that doesn't fax.
			}
		}
		```

		The `InkjetPrinter` is forced to implement methods it doesn't need, violating ISP. Instead, we can separate the interfaces:

		```java
		public interface Printer {
			void print();
		}

		public interface Scanner {
			void scan();
		}

		public interface FaxMachine {
			void fax();
		}

		public class LaserPrinter implements Printer, Scanner, FaxMachine {
			public void print() { /* Print document */ }
			public void scan() { /* Scan document */ }
			public void fax() { /* Fax document */ }
		}

		public class InkjetPrinter implements Printer {
			public void print() { /* Print document */ }
		}
		```

		Now, `InkjetPrinter` only implements the `Printer` interface, adhering to the Interface Segregation Principle.

		---

		### 5. **Dependency Inversion Principle (DIP)**

		#### **Definition:**
		High-level modules should not depend on low-level modules. Both should depend on abstractions. Additionally, abstractions should not depend on details. Details should depend on abstractions.

		#### **Real-World Example:**
		In a **Payment System**, a high-level class might directly depend on a low-level class for payment processing. For instance:

		```java
		public class PaymentProcessor {
			private CreditCardPayment creditCardPayment;

			public PaymentProcessor() {
				creditCardPayment = new CreditCardPayment();
			}

			public void processPayment() {
				creditCardPayment.process();
			}
		}
		```

		Here, the `PaymentProcessor` is tightly coupled to `CreditCardPayment`, violating DIP. Instead, we can abstract the payment processing:

		```java
		public interface PaymentMethod {
			void process();
		}

		public class CreditCardPayment implements PaymentMethod {
			public void process() {
				// Process credit card payment
			}
		}

		public class PaymentProcessor {
			private PaymentMethod paymentMethod;

			public PaymentProcessor(PaymentMethod paymentMethod) {
				this.paymentMethod = paymentMethod;
			}

			public void processPayment() {
				paymentMethod.process();
			}
		}
		```

		Now, `PaymentProcessor` is not dependent on any specific payment method. You can inject any payment method, such as `PayPalPayment`, making it flexible and adhering to DIP.

		---

		### Summary of SOLID Principles

		1. **SRP (Single Responsibility Principle)**: A class should have only one responsibility.
		2. **OCP (Open/Closed Principle)**: A class should be open for extension but closed for modification.
		3. **LSP (Liskov Substitution Principle)**: Subtypes must be substitutable for their base types.
		4. **ISP (Interface Segregation Principle)**: Don't force clients to implement interfaces they don't need.
		5. **DIP (Dependency Inversion Principle)**: High-level modules should depend on abstractions, not concrete classes.

		By following these SOLID principles, you can create maintainable, flexible, and scalable software.

========================
DESIGN PATTERNS IN JAVA
========================

	Design patterns are common solutions to recurring design problems in object-oriented software development. They help in making code more reusable, flexible, and easier to maintain.

	Creational Patterns
	========================

		1. **Singleton Pattern**

		   * **Use**: The Singleton pattern ensures that a class has only one instance and provides a global point of access to that instance.
		   * **When to Use**: Use it when you want to restrict instantiation of a class to a single object, such as in case of managing database connections or configuration settings.

		   **Example**:

		   ```java
		   public class Singleton {
			   private static Singleton instance;

			   private Singleton() {}

			   public static Singleton getInstance() {
				   if (instance == null) {
					   instance = new Singleton();
				   }
				   return instance;
			   }
		   }
		   ```

		2. **Factory Pattern**

		   * **Use**: The Factory Design Pattern is used to create objects without specifying the exact class of object that will be created. 
					  It defines a method for creating objects, allowing subclasses or other parts of the application to decide which class to instantiate.
					  
		   * **When to Use**: Use it when you don't know in advance what class objects you need to create or want to delegate the creation logic to subclasses.

		   **Example**:

		   ```java
		   interface Animal {
			   void speak();
		   }

		   class Dog implements Animal {
			   public void speak() {
				   System.out.println("Woof");
			   }
		   }

		   class Cat implements Animal {
			   public void speak() {
				   System.out.println("Meow");
			   }
		   }

		   class AnimalFactory {
			   public Animal createAnimal(String type) {
				   if(type.equals("dog")) {
					   return new Dog();
				   } else if(type.equals("cat")) {
					   return new Cat();
				   }
				   return null;
			   }
		   }
		   class Main {
			   public static void main(String[] args){
					AnimalFactory af = new AnimalFactory();
					Animal animal = af.createAnimal("cat");
					animal.speak(); // cat speak method will be called.
			   }
		   }
		   ```

		3. **Abstract Factory Pattern**

		   * **Use**: The Abstract Factory pattern provides an interface for creating families of related or dependent objects without specifying their concrete classes.
		   * **When to Use**: Use it when the client needs to create objects that belong to a particular family or type and wants to ensure the objects are compatible.

		   **Example**:

				### ✅ Code Structure: Notification System (SMS & Email)

				```java
				// Notification interface
				interface Notification {
					void send(String message);
				}

				// SMS Notification implementation
				class SMSNotification implements Notification {
					public void send(String message) {
						System.out.println("Sending SMS: " + message);
					}
				}

				// Email Notification implementation
				class EmailNotification implements Notification {
					public void send(String message) {
						System.out.println("Sending Email: " + message);
					}
				}

				// Abstract Factory
				interface NotificationFactory {
					Notification createNotification();
				}

				// SMS Factory
				class SMSNotificationFactory implements NotificationFactory {
					public Notification createNotification() {
						return new SMSNotification();
					}
				}

				// Email Factory
				class EmailNotificationFactory implements NotificationFactory {
					public Notification createNotification() {
						return new EmailNotification();
					}
				}

				// Main class to test the pattern
				public class Main {
					public static void main(String[] args) {
						// Use SMS notification
						NotificationFactory smsFactory = new SMSNotificationFactory();
						Notification sms = smsFactory.createNotification();
						sms.send("Your OTP is 123456");

						// Use Email notification
						NotificationFactory emailFactory = new EmailNotificationFactory();
						Notification email = emailFactory.createNotification();
						email.send("Welcome to our service!");
					}
				}
				```

				---

				### 🧩 Output:

				```
				Sending SMS: Your OTP is 123456
				Sending Email: Welcome to our service!
				```

		4. **Builder Pattern**

		   * **Use**: The Builder pattern separates the construction of a complex object from its representation, allowing the same construction process to create different representations.
		   * **When to Use**: Use it when you need to create an object with many optional parts, especially if some of the parts need to be set in a specific order.

		   **Example**:

		   ```java
				public class User {
					// Required parameters
					private final String username;
					private final String email;

					// Optional parameters
					private final int age;
					private final String phone;

					// Private constructor that accepts builder
					private User(UserBuilder builder) {
						this.username = builder.username;
						this.email = builder.email;
						this.age = builder.age;
						this.phone = builder.phone;
					}

					// Static inner Builder class
					public static class UserBuilder {
						// Required parameters
						private final String username;
						private final String email;

						// Optional parameters - initialized to default values
						private int age = 0;
						private String phone = "";

						// Builder constructor with required fields
						public UserBuilder(String username, String email) {
							this.username = username;
							this.email = email;
						}

						// Setter-like methods that return the builder itself
						public UserBuilder age(int age) {
							this.age = age;
							return this;
						}

						public UserBuilder phone(String phone) {
							this.phone = phone;
							return this;
						}

						// Build method that returns the final object
						public User build() {
							return new User(this);
						}
					}

					// Getters (optional, for accessing fields)
					public String getUsername() { return username; }
					public String getEmail() { return email; }
					public int getAge() { return age; }
					public String getPhone() { return phone; }
				}
				
				public class Main {
					public static void main(String[] args) {
						User user = new User.UserBuilder("nani", "nani@example.com")
										.age(30)
										.phone("9876543210")
										.build();

						System.out.println(user.getUsername()); // nani
					}
				}

		   ```

	Structural Patterns
	=====================

		1. **Adapter Pattern**

		   * **Use**: The Adapter pattern allows two incompatible interfaces to work together by creating an intermediary (adapter) that converts one interface into another.
		   * **When to Use**: Use it when you want to integrate two incompatible systems or classes that cannot communicate directly.

		   **Example**:

		   ```java
				package company.com.design_patterns.structural;

				// Existing interface used in your system
				interface PaymentProcessor {
					void pay(double amount);
				}

				// Third-party class with incompatible interface
				class PayPalAPI {
					public void makePayment(double amountInUSD) {
						System.out.println("Payment of $" + amountInUSD + " made through PayPal.");
					}
				}

				// Adapter class to bridge between your interface and PayPalAPI
				class PayPalAdapter implements PaymentProcessor {

					private final PayPalAPI payPalAPI;

					public PayPalAdapter(PayPalAPI payPalAPI) {
						this.payPalAPI = payPalAPI;
					}

					@Override
					public void pay(double amount) {
						payPalAPI.makePayment(amount);
					}
				}

				public class AdapterPatternExample {
					public static void main(String[] args) {

						PayPalAPI payPalAPI  = new PayPalAPI();
						PaymentProcessor paymentProcessor = new PayPalAdapter(payPalAPI);
						paymentProcessor.pay(200);
					}
				}
		   ```

		2. **Decorator Pattern**

		   * **Use**: The Decorator pattern allows you to add new behavior to an object without altering its structure. It enables dynamic enhancement of functionality at runtime. (OPEN/CLOSE Principle)
		   * **When to Use**: Use it when you need to extend the functionalities of a class in a flexible and reusable way.

		   **Example**:

		   ```java
				package company.com.design_patterns.structural;

				interface Notification {
					void send(String message);
				}

				class BasicNotification implements Notification {
					@Override
					public void send(String message) {
						System.out.println("Sending basic notification: " + message);
					}
				}

				class NotificationDecorator implements Notification {
					protected Notification notification;

					public NotificationDecorator(Notification notification) {
						this.notification = notification;
					}

					@Override
					public void send(String message) {
						notification.send(message);
					}
				}

				class SMSNotification extends NotificationDecorator {
					public SMSNotification(Notification notification) {
						super(notification);
					}

					@Override
					public void send(String message) {
						super.send(message);  // Send basic notification first
						System.out.println("Sending SMS notification: " + message);  // Add SMS functionality
					}
				}

				class EmailNotification extends NotificationDecorator {
					public EmailNotification(Notification notification) {
						super(notification);
					}

					@Override
					public void send(String message) {
						super.send(message);  // Send basic notification first
						System.out.println("Sending Email notification: " + message);  // Add Email functionality
					}
				}


				public class DecoratorPatternExample {
					public static void main(String[] args) {
						Notification notification = new BasicNotification();  // Basic notification

						// Add SMS functionality to the notification
						notification = new SMSNotification(notification);

						// Add Email functionality to the notification
						notification = new EmailNotification(notification);

						// Final notification that now includes SMS and Email
						notification.send("Hello, this is a test message!");
					}
				}
				
		   ```

		3. **Facade Pattern**

		   * **Use**: The Facade pattern provides a simplified interface to a complex system of classes, libraries, or frameworks.
		   * **When to Use**: Instead of interacting with every part of a system individually, you use the Facade to interact with the system in a simpler way.

		   **Example**:

		   ```java
			package company.com.design_patterns.structural;

			class Engine {
				void start() {
					System.out.println("Engine started");
				}
			}

			class Radio {
				void turnOn() {
					System.out.println("Radio turned on");
				}
			}

			class AirConditioning {
				void turnOn() {
					System.out.println("Air conditioning turned on");
				}
			}

			class CarFacade {
				private Engine engine;
				private Radio radio;
				private AirConditioning ac;

				CarFacade() {
					engine = new Engine();
					radio = new Radio();
					ac = new AirConditioning();
				}

				void startCar() {
					engine.start();
					radio.turnOn();
					ac.turnOn();
				}
			}

			public class FacadePatternExample {
				public static void main(String[] args) {
					CarFacade car = new CarFacade();
					car.startCar();
				}
			}
		   ```

	Behavioral Patterns
	====================
		1. **Observer Pattern**

		   * **Use**: The Observer pattern defines a dependency between objects so that when one object changes state, all its dependents are notified and updated automatically.
		   * **When to Use**: Use it when you want to establish a one-to-many dependency between objects, such as in event handling or data binding.
							When a YouTube channel uploads a new video, all subscribed users get a notification.


		   **Example**:

		   ```java
				package company.com.design_patterns.behavioral;

				import java.util.ArrayList;
				import java.util.List;

				interface Subscriber{
					void update(String videoTitle);
				}
				class User implements Subscriber{
					private String name;

					public User(String name){
						this.name = name;
					}

					@Override
					public void update(String videoTitle) {
						System.out.println(name+" got notified: New video uploaded : "+videoTitle);
					}
				}
				class YoutubeChannel{
					private List<Subscriber> subscribers = new ArrayList<>();

					public void subscribe(Subscriber subscriber){
						subscribers.add(subscriber);
					}

					public void uploadNewVideo(String title){
						System.out.println("YouTube Channel uploaded: " + title);
						notifySubscribers(title);
					}

					private  void notifySubscribers(String title){
						for(Subscriber subscriber : subscribers){
							subscriber.update(title);
						}
					}
				}
				public class ObserverPatternExample{
					public static void main(String[] args) {

						System.out.println("\n");

						YoutubeChannel channel = new YoutubeChannel();

						Subscriber user1 = new User("Alice");
						Subscriber user2 = new User("Bob");

						channel.subscribe(user1);
						channel.subscribe(user2);

						channel.uploadNewVideo("Learn AWS course in 10 minutes");

						System.out.println("\n");
					}
				}
		   ```

		2. **Strategy Pattern**

		   * **Use**: The Strategy pattern allows you to define a family of algorithms, encapsulate each one, and make them interchangeable.
		   * **When to Use**: Use it when you have multiple ways of performing a task and want to switch between them dynamically.

		   **Example**:

		   ```java
		   interface PaymentStrategy {
			   void pay(int amount);
		   }

		   class CreditCardPayment implements PaymentStrategy {
			   public void pay(int amount) {
				   System.out.println("Paid " + amount + " using Credit Card");
			   }
		   }

		   class PayPalPayment implements PaymentStrategy {
			   public void pay(int amount) {
				   System.out.println("Paid " + amount + " using PayPal");
			   }
		   }

		   class PaymentContext {
			   private PaymentStrategy strategy;

			   public void setPaymentStrategy(PaymentStrategy strategy) {
				   this.strategy = strategy;
			   }

			   public void executePayment(int amount) {
				   strategy.pay(amount);
			   }
		   }
		   ```

		3. **Command Pattern**

		   * **Use**: The Command pattern turns a request into a stand-alone object that contains all information about the request. It allows for parameterization of objects with operations.
		   * **When to Use**: Use it when you need to decouple sender and receiver objects, or if you need to queue requests or log them for undo operations.

		   **Example**:

		   ```java
		   interface Command {
			   void execute();
		   }

		   class LightOnCommand implements Command {
			   private Light light;

			   public LightOnCommand(Light light) {
				   this.light = light;
			   }

			   public void execute() {
				   light.turnOn();
			   }
		   }

		   class Light {
			   public void turnOn() {
				   System.out.println("Light is on");
			   }

			   public void turnOff() {
				   System.out.println("Light is off");
			   }
		   }

		   class RemoteControl {
			   private Command command;

			   public void setCommand(Command command) {
				   this.command = command;
			   }

			   public void pressButton() {
				   command.execute();
			   }
		   }
		   ```

		---




		=============================
		MICROSERVICES DESIGN PATTERN
		=============================
		---

		1. GATEWAY PATTERN

		Purpose: Acts as a single entry point for all client requests and routes them to the appropriate microservices. It also handles cross-cutting concerns like authentication, logging, and rate-limiting.

		Spring Boot Tool: Spring Cloud Gateway

		Example:

		```
		# application.yml (API Gateway)
		spring:
		  cloud:
			gateway:
			  routes:
				- id: user-service
				  uri: http://localhost:8081/
				  predicates:
					- Path=/users/**
		```

		Use Case: Instead of calling each microservice directly, clients talk to the API Gateway. It forwards requests to services dynamically.

		---

		2. SERVICE REGISTRY PATTERN

		Purpose: Allows microservices to register themselves and discover other services at runtime instead of hardcoding URLs.

		Spring Boot Tool: Eureka Server (Spring Cloud Netflix Eureka)

		Example:

		```java
		// Eureka Server
		@EnableEurekaServer
		@SpringBootApplication
		public class EurekaServerApp { }
		```

		```java
		// Microservice client
		@EnableEurekaClient
		@SpringBootApplication
		public class UserServiceApp { }
		```

		Use Case: Services register and discover each other dynamically via Eureka, enabling load balancing and failover.

		---

		3. CIRCUIT BREAKER PATTERN

		Purpose: Protects the system from cascading failures when a service is down by stopping further requests temporarily.
				 If you keep calling that failing service, it wastes resources and makes your system slower.
				 Instead, it returns a fallback response or error immediately.

		Spring Boot Tool: Resilience4j or Spring Cloud Circuit Breaker

		Example:

		```java
		@CircuitBreaker(name = "userService", fallbackMethod = "fallbackMethod")
		public String getUserDetails() {
			return restTemplate.getForObject("http://USER-SERVICE/users", String.class);
		}

		public String fallbackMethod(Exception e) {
			return "User service is temporarily unavailable.";
		}
		```

		Use Case: Prevents repeated failures when one service is not responding, and provides a fallback response.

		---

		4. SAGA PATTERN

		Purpose: Manages distributed transactions in microservices using either orchestration or choreography.
		
		The Saga Pattern is a design pattern to manage distributed transactions across multiple microservices.
		It helps maintain data consistency in a distributed system without using traditional ACID transactions (which are hard to implement across services).

		- What Saga does:
		  - It breaks a big task into small steps.
		  - Each step happens one by one, and each step is done by a different microservice.
		  - If everything goes well, all steps complete successfully.
		  - If something goes wrong at any step, Saga makes sure to undo (rollback) the previous steps so the system doesn’t get messed up.
			- Example Use Case
			Imagine an e-commerce order process involving:

			Order Service (creates order)

			Payment Service (processes payment)

			Inventory Service (reserves stock)

			Shipping Service (arranges delivery)

			Each step is a local transaction. If payment fails, the saga triggers compensations like cancelling order or releasing inventory.

		Example (Event-based Saga):

		* Order Service publishes "Order Created" event to Kafka
		* Payment Service consumes the event and processes payment
		* If payment fails, it emits "Payment Failed"
		* Order Service listens and performs compensating actions

		Use Case: Maintains data consistency across multiple services without using distributed transactions.

		---

		5. CQRS (COMMAND QUERY RESPONSIBILITY SEGREGATION)

		Purpose: Separates the data model used to update data (commands) from the model used to read data (queries).

		Spring Boot Implementation: Use different models/services/controllers for read and write operations.

		Example:

		```java
		// Command Controller
		@PostMapping("/orders")
		public void createOrder(@RequestBody OrderRequest request) {
			orderService.createOrder(request);
		}

		// Query Controller
		@GetMapping("/orders/{id}")
		public OrderDTO getOrder(@PathVariable String id) {
			return orderService.getOrderById(id);
		}
		```

		Use Case: Improves performance and scalability by optimizing read and write operations separately.

		---

		6. API COMPOSITION

		Purpose: Combines data from multiple microservices into a single unified response for the client.

		Spring Boot Implementation: Use API Gateway or a separate aggregator service to fetch data from other services.

		Example:

		```java
		public OrderDetails getOrderDetails(String orderId) {
			Order order = orderService.getOrder(orderId);
			Payment payment = paymentService.getPaymentByOrderId(orderId);
			return new OrderDetails(order, payment);
		}
		```

		Use Case: Client receives complete information in a single API call instead of calling multiple services.

		---

		7. EVENT-DRIVEN ARCHITECTURE

		Purpose: Microservices communicate asynchronously by publishing and consuming events.

		Spring Boot Tool: Kafka, RabbitMQ, or Spring Cloud Stream

		Example:

		```java
		// Producer
		kafkaTemplate.send("order-events", new OrderCreatedEvent(order));

		// Consumer
		@KafkaListener(topics = "order-events")
		public void handleOrderEvent(OrderCreatedEvent event) {
			// process event
		}
		```

		Use Case: Services are loosely coupled and can react to changes in other services asynchronously.

		---

		8. DATABASE PER SERVICE

		Purpose: Each microservice manages its own database, ensuring independence and encapsulation.

		Spring Boot Implementation: Configure a dedicated database connection in each microservice.

		Example:

		```
		spring:
		  datasource:
			url: jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/orderdb
			username: root
			password: password
		```

		Use Case: Allows each service to evolve independently without affecting others.

		---

		Sure! Here's the Retry Pattern in **plain text** format, using technical terms explained in a simple way — no bold styling, just clean and clear content.

		---

		9. RETRY PATTERN

		Purpose: Automatically retries failed service calls that fail due to temporary problems like network issues, slow responses, or momentary unavailability. This pattern helps improve system stability and avoids failure due to short-lived issues.

		Spring Boot Tool: Spring Retry or Resilience4j Retry

		Example:

		```java
		// Using Resilience4j Retry with Spring Boot

		@Retry(name = "retryService", fallbackMethod = "fallbackMethod")
		public String callExternalService() {
			// This method calls another microservice or external API
			return restTemplate.getForObject("http://external-service/api/data", String.class);
		}

		// Fallback method when all retry attempts fail
		public String fallbackMethod(Exception e) {
			return "Service temporarily unavailable. Please try again later.";
		}
		```

		application.yml:

		```yaml
		resilience4j.retry:
		  instances:
			retryService:
			  max-attempts: 3
			  wait-duration: 2s
		```

		Use Case: Suppose the order service tries to call the payment service, but due to a temporary issue (like a network timeout), it fails. Instead of failing immediately, the retry pattern automatically tries again up to 3 times. If all attempts fail, it returns a fallback message. This helps avoid unnecessary errors and provides a better experience for users.

		---

============================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================
ANGULAR AND REACT - FRONT-END
===================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================
ANGULAR VERSION - version-14v
REACT VERSION - version-16.8v

		MICRO-FRONT-END
		===============
		---

		**Monolithic Frontend Architecture vs Micro Frontends Using React.js**
		----------------------------------------------------------------------
		---

		### 1. Monolithic Frontend Architecture Using React

		- A monolithic frontend is a single React application that contains all features, pages, modules, and logic in one codebase. 
		- In Monolithic Architecture, Multiple components are combined into one large application.
		- All modules are reside in single repository and we have single CI/CD pipeline to deploy all the changes.
		- It is deployed as a single unit.

		**Example Folder Structure:**

		```
		src/
		├── components/
		├── pages/
		├── services/
		├── App.jsx
		├── index.js
		```

		**Advantages:**

		* Simple setup: Easy to scaffold and develop using Create React App or Vite.
		* Single deployment: One build and one deployment pipeline.
		* Centralized state management: Easy to use tools like Redux, Context API, or Zustand.

		**Disadvantages:**

		* Scaling limitations: Becomes difficult to manage with a growing number of developers or features.
		* Slower build times: Larger apps lead to longer build and deploy times.
		* Tight coupling: Hard to isolate features or allow team-level autonomy.

		---

		### 2. Micro Frontend Architecture Using React
		----------------------------------------------
		- Micro front-ends are implemented on the base of micro services from backend.
		- Micro frontends divide a large React application into smaller, independently developed and deployed applications. 
		- They can have separate repository , CI/CD pipelines for managing codebase and deployement.
		- Each micro frontend is responsible for a specific feature or domain and is usually managed by a dedicated team.
		- Each micro frontend can be developed using its own technology stack (React, Angular, Vue.js, etc.).
		- Micro frontends are integrated into a single user interface at runtime (via iframe, Webpack Module Federation, or Single-SPA).
		
		**Example Folder Structure:**

		```
		micro-frontends/
		├── shell(or)container/    ← Main host app (handles routing and layout)
		├── user-dashboard/        ← Micro frontend 1
		├── policy-management/     ← Micro frontend 2
		├── claim-status/          ← Micro frontend 3
		```

		**Common Tools Used:**

		* Webpack Module Federation
		* Single-SPA
		* Qiankun
		* Nx (monorepo management)
		* Bit.dev (component sharing)

		**Advantages:**

		* Independent deployments: Each team can build and deploy its part of the app separately.
		* Scalable architecture: Suited for large applications with multiple teams.
		* Improved fault isolation: A failure in one micro frontend does not crash the entire application.

		**Disadvantages:**

		* Complex setup: Requires more advanced configuration for routing, communication, and deployment.
		* Risk of code duplication: Especially for shared dependencies if not handled properly.
		* Global state sharing: Requires patterns like pub-sub or shared libraries for cross-microfrontend communication.

		---

		### 3. Monolith vs Micro Frontends in React

		| Feature            | Monolithic React App            | Micro Frontends (React)               |
		| ------------------ | ------------------------------- | ------------------------------------- |
		| Codebase           | Single                          | Multiple (per feature or team)        |
		| Deployment         | One build and deploy            | Independent deployments               |
		| Team Collaboration | Shared codebase (less scalable) | Team autonomy (more scalable)         |
		| State Management   | Centralized                     | Needs shared or decoupled solutions   |
		| Performance        | Slower for large apps           | Faster for smaller, isolated apps     |
		| DevOps / CI-CD     | Simple                          | Complex (per micro frontend pipeline) |

		---

		### 4. When to Use What?

		**Use Monolithic Frontend When:**

		* Building small to medium-sized projects.
		* You have a small or single development team.
		* You need to deliver a quick MVP or short-term project.
		* You prefer a simple CI/CD setup.

		**Use Micro Frontends When:**

		* Working on large enterprise-scale applications.
		* Multiple independent teams are involved.
		* You need independent deployments and modular scaling.
		* You are planning a long-term, scalable project architecture.

		---

		### 5. Recommended React Tools and Libraries

		**For Monolithic Architecture:**

		* React Router
		* Redux / Context API / Zustand
		* React Query
		* Create React App or Vite

		**For Micro Frontends Architecture:**

		* Webpack 5 Module Federation
		* Single-SPA
		* Qiankun
		* Nx (monorepo support)
		* Bit.dev (shared components)

		---

			SAMPLE PROJECT FOR MICRO-FRONT-END ORDER MANAGEMENT 
			===================================================
			---

			# Micro Frontend Architecture using React for Order Management System

			Micro Frontend Services:

			* Customer Service – [http://localhost:3000](http://localhost:3000)
			* Order Service – [http://localhost:3001](http://localhost:3001)
			* Product Service – [http://localhost:3002](http://localhost:3002)

			Unified via Container App/Shell

			## High-Level Solution

			We’ll use Module Federation from Webpack 5 to stitch together micro frontends.

			Architecture:

			* Container App (Shell): Loads and displays all the micro frontends.
			* Each Micro Frontend (customer, order, product) is deployed separately.
			* All services expose components via Module Federation.
			* The container consumes and renders them dynamically.

			## Technologies Used

			* React
			* Webpack 5
			* Module Federation
			* Separate repositories + deployments
			* Single routing shell app

			## Project Structure

			microfrontend-root/
			├── container/          (Main shell app)
			├── customer/           (Micro frontend - Customer)
			├── orders/             (Micro frontend - Order)
			└── products/           (Micro frontend - Product)

			1. Micro Frontend Setup (Customer, Orders, Products)

			---

			Each micro frontend will:

			* Have its own webpack.config.js
			* Expose components via Module Federation
			* Run on its own port

			## Example: customer/ Micro Frontend

			customer/package.json

			```json
			{
			  "name": "customer",
			  "scripts": {
				"start": "webpack serve --mode development --port 3000"
			  },
			  "dependencies": {
				"react": "^18.0.0",
				"react-dom": "^18.0.0"
			  },
			  "devDependencies": {
				"webpack": "^5",
				"webpack-cli": "^4",
				"webpack-dev-server": "^4",
				"html-webpack-plugin": "^5",
				"babel-loader": "^8"
			  }
			}
			```

			customer/webpack.config.js

			```js
			const HtmlWebpackPlugin = require('html-webpack-plugin');
			const ModuleFederationPlugin = require('webpack/lib/container/ModuleFederationPlugin');

			module.exports = {
			  entry: './src/index.js',
			  mode: 'development',
			  devServer: {
				port: 3000
			  },
			  output: {
				publicPath: 'auto'
			  },
			  module: {
				rules: [
				  {
					test: /\.js$/,
					loader: 'babel-loader',
					exclude: /node_modules/
				  }
				]
			  },
			  plugins: [
				new ModuleFederationPlugin({
				  name: 'customer',
				  filename: 'remoteEntry.js',
				  exposes: {
					'./CustomerApp': './src/CustomerApp'
				  },
				  shared: { react: { singleton: true }, 'react-dom': { singleton: true } }
				}),
				new HtmlWebpackPlugin({
				  template: './public/index.html'
				})
			  ]
			}
			```

			customer/src/index.js

			```js
			import('./CustomerApp');
			```

			customer/src/CustomerApp.js

			```js
			import React from 'react';
			import ReactDOM from 'react-dom';

			const App = () => <div>🧑 Customer Service Component</div>;

			ReactDOM.render(<App />, document.getElementById('root'));

			export default App;
			```

			Repeat similar setup for orders/ on port 3001 and products/ on 3002.
			Replace './CustomerApp' with './OrderApp' or './ProductApp' accordingly.

			2. Container App (Main Shell UI)

			---

			container/package.json

			```json
			{
			  "name": "container",
			  "scripts": {
				"start": "webpack serve --mode development --port 8080"
			  },
			  "dependencies": {
				"react": "^18.0.0",
				"react-dom": "^18.0.0",
				"react-router-dom": "^6.0.0"
			  },
			  "devDependencies": {
				"webpack": "^5",
				"webpack-cli": "^4",
				"webpack-dev-server": "^4",
				"html-webpack-plugin": "^5",
				"babel-loader": "^8"
			  }
			}
			```

			container/webpack.config.js

			```js
			const HtmlWebpackPlugin = require('html-webpack-plugin');
			const ModuleFederationPlugin = require('webpack/lib/container/ModuleFederationPlugin');

			module.exports = {
			  entry: './src/index.js',
			  mode: 'development',
			  devServer: {
				port: 8080
			  },
			  output: {
				publicPath: 'auto'
			  },
			  module: {
				rules: [
				  {
					test: /\.js$/,
					loader: 'babel-loader',
					exclude: /node_modules/
				  }
				]
			  },
			  plugins: [
				new ModuleFederationPlugin({
				  name: 'container',
				  remotes: {
					customer: 'customer@http://localhost:3000/remoteEntry.js',
					orders: 'orders@http://localhost:3001/remoteEntry.js',
					products: 'products@http://localhost:3002/remoteEntry.js'
				  },
				  shared: { react: { singleton: true }, 'react-dom': { singleton: true } }
				}),
				new HtmlWebpackPlugin({
				  template: './public/index.html'
				})
			  ]
			}
			```

			container/src/index.js

			```js
			import React from 'react';
			import ReactDOM from 'react-dom/client';
			import App from './App';

			const root = ReactDOM.createRoot(document.getElementById('root'));
			root.render(<App />);
			```

			container/src/App.js

			```js
			import React, { Suspense } from 'react';
			import { BrowserRouter, Routes, Route, Link } from 'react-router-dom';

			const CustomerApp = React.lazy(() => import('customer/CustomerApp'));
			const OrderApp = React.lazy(() => import('orders/OrderApp'));
			const ProductApp = React.lazy(() => import('products/ProductApp'));

			const App = () => (
			  <BrowserRouter>
				<nav>
				  <Link to="/">Home</Link> | 
				  <Link to="/customer">Customer</Link> | 
				  <Link to="/order">Order</Link> | 
				  <Link to="/product">Product</Link>
				</nav>

				<Suspense fallback={<div>Loading...</div>}>
				  <Routes>
					<Route path="/" element={<div>🏠 Home - Order Management System</div>} />
					<Route path="/customer" element={<CustomerApp />} />
					<Route path="/order" element={<OrderApp />} />
					<Route path="/product" element={<ProductApp />} />
				  </Routes>
				</Suspense>
			  </BrowserRouter>
			);

			export default App;
			```

			container/public/index.html

			```html
			<!DOCTYPE html>
			<html>
			  <head><title>Micro Frontend Container</title></head>
			  <body>
				<div id="root"></div>
			  </body>
			</html>
			```

			## Run All Apps

			1. Start micro frontends:

			```bash
			cd customer && npm install && npm start  
			cd orders && npm install && npm start  
			cd products && npm install && npm start
			```

			2. Start container:

			```bash
			cd container && npm install && npm start
			```

			## Final Result

			Unified UI accessible at: [http://localhost:8080](http://localhost:8080)

			* /customer → renders CustomerApp from [http://localhost:3000](http://localhost:3000)
			* /order → renders OrderApp from [http://localhost:3001](http://localhost:3001)
			* /product → renders ProductApp from [http://localhost:3002](http://localhost:3002)


			
		
		========================================================================================================================================================================================

		ANGULAR TUTORIAL 
		========================================================================================================================================================================================

		Here's the Angular tutorial structured with theory followed by the code examples, as per your request:

		---

		### **angular: overview**

		Angular is a framework used to build dynamic web applications using TypeScript. It supports components, routing, forms, services, dependency injection, and observables.

		---

		### **setup: install**

		To start an Angular project, install the Angular CLI, create a new project using `ng new`, and serve it using `ng serve`.

		**Code:**

		```bash
		npm install -g @angular/cli
		ng new my-app
		cd my-app
		ng serve
		```

		---

		### **component: creation**

		A component is the basic building block of an Angular app. It contains HTML, CSS, and logic in TypeScript. You can create it using the CLI.

		**Code:**

		```bash
		ng generate component my-component
		```

		---

		### **component: basic structure**

		A component has a selector (HTML tag), template or HTML file, and a class where we write business logic. The class contains properties and methods.

		**Code:**

		```ts
		import { Component } from '@angular/core';

		@Component({
		  selector: 'app-my-component',
		  templateUrl: './my-component.component.html',
		})
		export class MyComponent {
		  title = 'Hello Angular';
		}
		```

		---

		### **template: interpolation**

		Interpolation is used to display data from the component class into the HTML using double curly braces `{{}}`.

		**Code:**

		```html
		<h1>{{ title }}</h1>
		```

		---

		### **binding: types**

		#### **property binding:**

		It binds a property from the component to an element's property in HTML using square brackets `[ ]`.

		**Code:**

		```html
		<input [value]="title" />
		```

		#### **event binding:**

		It allows you to listen to user events like clicks and call methods using parentheses `( )`.

		**Code:**

		```html
		<button (click)="handleClick()">Click Me</button>
		```

		#### **two-way binding:**

		It combines property and event binding to update data in both component and template. It uses `[(ngModel)]` and requires FormsModule.

		**Code:**

		```html
		<input [(ngModel)]="title" />
		```

		---

		### **directive: types**

		#### **structural directives:**
