	===========================================================================================================================================================================================
		MYSQL VS MongoDB
		=================
		
		1. When to Use MySQL (Relational Database):
		------------------------------------------
		 # Scenario: Online Banking System (e.g., SBI, HDFC)

		* Project: Core Banking Application
		* Frontend: Angular or React for admin/customer portals
		* Backend: Spring Boot with Spring Security, Spring Data JPA
		* Database: MySQL

		 # Why MySQL:

		* You need strong relationships between tables (customers, accounts, transactions).
		* Data integrity is critical (foreign keys, constraints).
		* Complex JOINs are common (e.g., all transactions of a customer).
		* ACID compliance is mandatory (Atomicity, Consistency, Isolation, Durability).

		 # Example Tables:

		* `Customer(id, name, email)`
		* `Account(id, customer_id, type, balance)`
		* `Transaction(id, account_id, amount, timestamp)`

		 # Why Spring Boot + MySQL:

		* You can use Spring Data JPA with entities and repositories.
		* Relational data fits perfectly into Java's object model.
		* Easy to write queries using JPQL.

		Here is how the JSON document you shared would be represented in MySQL using a relational structure:
		 # Example Relation Sturcture:

		---

		  Table: `product`

		| id | product\_name | price |
		| -- | ------------- | ----- |
		| 1  | Laptop        | 45000 |

		---

		  Table: `specs`

		| id | product\_id | cpu | ram  | storage   |
		| -- | ----------- | --- | ---- | --------- |
		| 1  | 1           | i5  | 16GB | 512GB SSD |

		---

		  Table: `review`

		| id | product\_id | user | rating | comment  |
		| -- | ----------- | ---- | ------ | -------- |
		| 1  | 1           | Nani | 4      | Good one |

		---

		Each part of the JSON is broken into related tables with foreign key relationships:

		* `specs.product_id` → references `product.id`
		* `review.product_id` → references `product.id`

		This normalized design ensures proper data integrity in relational databases like MySQL.

		---

		2. When to Use MongoDB (NoSQL Database):
		------------------------------------------
		 # Scenario: E-commerce Catalog or Social Media Feed (e.g., Amazon, Instagram)

		* Project: Product Catalog / Social Media Feed
		* Frontend: React or Angular for user dashboard
		* Backend: Spring Boot REST API
		* Database: MongoDB

		 # Why MongoDB:

		* Data is unstructured or semi-structured (products, reviews, tags).
		* Frequent changes to data structure (e.g., adding new fields to products).
		* Needs high scalability and fast read/write.
		* Use of embedded documents and arrays (e.g., comments under posts).
		* Less need for complex JOINs; use `$lookup` only if needed.

		 # Example Documents:

		```json
		{
		  "productName": "Laptop",
		  "price": 45000,
		  "specs": {
			"cpu": "i5",
			"ram": "16GB",
			"storage": "512GB SSD"
		  },
		  "reviews": [
			{ "user": "Nani", "rating": 4, "comment": "Good one" }
		  ]
		}
		```

		 # Why Spring Boot + MongoDB:

		* Use Spring Data MongoDB with documents.
		* Fast data access using repositories.
		* Structure adapts to evolving data models (no migration needed).

		---

		  Summary:

		| Feature                  | MySQL (Relational)                           | MongoDB (NoSQL)                                  |
		| ------------------------ | -------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ |
		| Use Case                 | Banking, ERP, HRMS                           | E-commerce, Social Apps, IoT                     |
		| Data Structure           | Fixed schema, normalized                     | Flexible, JSON-like (documents)                  |
		| Query Style              | SQL with JOINs                               | JSON queries, Aggregations                       |
		| Scalability              | Vertical mostly                              | Horizontal (easy to scale)                       |
		| Example App              | Core Banking System                          | Product Catalog, News Feed                       |
		| Backend Integration      | Spring Boot + JPA                            | Spring Boot + MongoTemplate or Spring Data Mongo |
		| Frontend (Same for both) | Angular / React (API calls using Axios/Http) | Angular / React (API calls using Axios/Http)     |

		---

	===============================================================================================================================================================================================================

		  MongoDB Tutorial
		=====================
		Summary:
		----------
		Document = Record

		Collection = Table

		Embedded Document = Related Tables (with foreign keys in MySQL) - Employee Table With Address Table
		
		---

		1. What is MongoDB :
		   MongoDB is a NoSQL database that stores data in flexible, JSON-like documents.
		   Unlike SQL databases (like MySQL), MongoDB does not use tables and rows.
		   It is schema-less, which means each document can have a different structure.
		   It is used in modern applications where fast read and write of large datasets is needed.
		   ex: MongoDB stores employee records as documents instead of rows in a table.

		---

		2. Database :
		   A database is a container that holds collections.
		   You can create multiple databases in MongoDB.
		   Each database contains collections (like tables in SQL).
		   ex: A database named `company`.

		```js
		use company
		```

		---

		3. Collection :
		   A collection is a group of MongoDB documents.
		   It is similar to a table in SQL but doesn't have a fixed schema.
		   You can have multiple collections like `employees`, `addresses`, `departments`.
		   ex: Collection named `employees`.

		---

		4. Document :
		   A document is a single record in JSON (or BSON) format.
		   It can have embedded documents and arrays.
		   ex:

		```js
		{
		  "name": "Nani",
		  "age": 30,
		  "designation": "Developer"
		}
		```

		---

		5. Insert One Document :
		   Use `insertOne()` to insert a single document into a collection.
		   ex:

		```js
		db.employees.insertOne({
		  name: "Nani",
		  age: 30,
		  designation: "Developer"
		})
		```

		---

		6. Insert Many Documents :
		   Use `insertMany()` to insert multiple documents at once.
		   ex:

		```js
		db.employees.insertMany([
		  { name: "Ravi", age: 28, designation: "Tester" },
		  { name: "Anu", age: 26, designation: "HR" }
		])
		```

		---

		7. Show All Collections :
		   Use `show collections` to list all collections in the current database.
		   ex:

		```js
		show collections
		```

		---

		8. Find All Documents :
		   Use `find()` to get all documents in a collection.
		   ex:

		```js
		db.employees.find()
		```

		---

		9. Find With Condition :
		   Use a query filter inside `find()` to match specific fields.
		   ex:

		```js
		db.employees.find({ designation: "Developer" })
		```

		---

		10. Update One Document :
			Use `updateOne()` to update one matching document.
			Use `$set` to set new values.
			ex:

		```js
		db.employees.updateOne(
		  { name: "Nani" },
		  { $set: { age: 31 } }
		)
		```

		---

		11. Update Many Documents :
			Use `updateMany()` to update all documents that match a condition.
			ex:

		```js
		db.employees.updateMany(
		  { designation: "Tester" },
		  { $set: { department: "QA" } }
		)
		```

		---

		12. Delete One Document :
			Use `deleteOne()` to remove one document.
			ex:

		```js
		db.employees.deleteOne({ name: "Anu" })
		```

		---

		13. Delete Many Documents :
			Use `deleteMany()` to delete multiple documents.
			ex:

		```js
		db.employees.deleteMany({ designation: "Intern" })
		```

		---

		14. Drop a Collection :
			Use `drop()` to delete an entire collection.
			ex:

		```js
		db.employees.drop()
		```

		---

		15. Drop a Database :
			Use `db.dropDatabase()` to delete the current database.
			ex:

		```js
		db.dropDatabase()
		```

		---

		16. Embedded Documents :
			You can store related data inside a document using nested fields.
			ex: Store address inside employee document.

		```js
		db.employees.insertOne({
		  name: "Nani",
		  age: 30,
		  designation: "Developer",
		  address: {
			city: "Hyderabad",
			pincode: 500001
		  }
		})
		```

		---

		17. Arrays in Documents :
			You can store multiple values in an array field.
			ex:

		```js
		db.employees.insertOne({
		  name: "Ravi",
		  skills: ["Java", "Spring Boot", "MongoDB"]
		})
		```

		---

		18. Create Index :
			Indexes help improve performance of search queries.
			ex:

		```js
		db.employees.createIndex({ name: 1 })
		```

		---

		19. Relationships Using Manual References :
			You can store `addressId` in employee document and match it with `_id` in `addresses` collection.
			This is like a foreign key in SQL.
			ex:
			In addresses collection:

		```js
		db.addresses.insertOne({
		  _id: 1,
		  city: "Hyderabad",
		  state: "Telangana"
		})
		```

		In employees collection:

		```js
		db.employees.insertOne({
		  name: "Kiran",
		  addressId: 1
		})
		```

		You can join them in code using two queries or in aggregation with `$lookup`.

		---

		20. Aggregation Example :
			Aggregation is used to perform operations like sum, average, count, etc.
			ex:

		```js
		db.employees.aggregate([
		  { $group: { _id: "$designation", total: { $sum: 1 } } }
		])
		```

		---

		21. Lookup (Join) Example :
			Use `$lookup` to join two collections.
			ex:

		```js
		db.employees.aggregate([
		  {
			$lookup: {
			  from: "addresses",
			  localField: "addressId",
			  foreignField: "_id",
			  as: "addressDetails"
			}
		  }
		])
		```

		---

		22. Export and Import Data :
			Use `mongoexport` to export data and `mongoimport` to import JSON or CSV files.
			ex:

		```bash
		mongoexport --db=company --collection=employees --out=employees.json
		mongoimport --db=company --collection=employees --file=employees.json
		```

		---

		23. GUI Tool :
			Use MongoDB Compass to visualize collections, documents, and run queries without the command line.

		---

		24. Comparison with SQL :
			| Feature           | MongoDB             | SQL (MySQL, etc.)     |
			|------------------|---------------------|------------------------|
			| Data Format       | JSON/BSON Documents | Rows and Columns       |
			| Schema            | Flexible            | Fixed                  |
			| Joins             | Manual or `$lookup` | Built-in JOINs         |
			| Scalability       | Easy (Horizontal)   | Harder (Vertical)      |
			| Performance       | Fast with Big Data  | Slower for large joins |


	=================================================================================================================================================================





		KAFKA Tutorial
		==============
		Apache Kafka 
		------------
		Apache Kafka is a software platform which is based on a distributed streaming process. 
		It is a publish-subscribe messaging system which let exchanging of data between applications, servers, and processors as well.

		What is a messaging system
		--------------------------
		A messaging system is a simple exchange of messages between two or more persons, devices, etc. A publish-subscribe messaging system allows a sender to send/write the message and a receiver to read that message. 
		In Apache Kafka, a sender is known as a producer who publishes messages, and a receiver is known as a consumer who consumes that message by subscribing it.

		What is Streaming process
		-------------------------
		A streaming process is the processing of data in parallelly connected systems. 
		This process allows different applications to limit the parallel execution of the data, where one record executes without waiting for the output of the previous record. 
		Therefore, a distributed streaming platform enables the user to simplify the task of the streaming process and parallel execution.

			Therefore, a streaming platform in Kafka has the following key capabilities:
			1. As soon as the streams of records occur, it processes it.
			2. It works similar to an enterprise messaging system where it publishes and subscribes streams of records.
			3. It stores the streams of records in a fault-tolerant durable way.
			
			
		Producer API: This API allows/permits an application to publish streams of records to one or more topics. (discussed in later section)

		Consumer API: This API allows an application to subscribe one or more topics and process the stream of records produced to them.

		Streams API: This API allows an application to effectively transform the input streams to the output streams. It permits an application to act as a stream processor which consumes an input stream from one or more topics, and produce an output stream to one or more output topics.

		Connector API: This API executes the reusable producer and consumer APIs with the existing data systems or applications.

		Why Apache Kafka
		----------------

		Apache Kafka is a software platform that has the following reasons which best describes the need of Apache Kafka.

		1. Apache Kafka is capable of handling millions of data or messages per second.

		2. Apache Kafka works as a mediator between the source system and the target system. 
		Thus, the source system (producer) data is sent to the Apache Kafka, where it decouples the data, and the target system (consumer) consumes the data from Kafka.

		3. Apache Kafka is having extremely high performance, i.e., it has really low latency value less than 10ms which proves it as a well-versed software.

		4. Apache Kafka has a resilient architecture which has resolved unusual complications in data sharing.

		5. Organizations such as NETFLIX, UBER, Walmart, etc. and over thousands of such firms make use of Apache Kafka.

		6. Apache Kafka is able to maintain the fault-tolerance. Fault-tolerance means that sometimes a consumer successfully consumes the message that was delivered by the producer. 
		But, the consumer fails to process the message back due to backend database failure, or due to presence of a bug in the consumer code. 
		In such a situation, the consumer is unable to consume the message again. Consequently, Apache Kafka has resolved the problem by reprocessing the data


		Kafka key Concepts 
		==================

		1. Producer: A producer is an application or service that sends data (messages or events) to a Kafka topic.
		
		2. Topic: A topic is a category or feed name where messages are published by producers and read by consumers. Topics help organize messages.
		
		3. Partition: Each topic can be divided into partitions. Partitions allow Kafka to scale horizontally and process data in parallel.
		
			Note : Scale horizontally means adding more machines or nodes to a system to handle increased load, instead of upgrading a single machine (which is called scaling vertically).

		4. Offset: An offset is a unique identifier assigned to each message in a partition. It helps keep track of the read position for consumers.
		
		5. Consumer: A consumer is an application or service that reads data from a Kafka topic.
		
		6. Consumer Group: A consumer group is a group of consumers that work together to consume messages from a topic. Each message is delivered to only one consumer in the group.
		
		7. Broker: A broker is a Kafka server that stores messages and serves them to consumers. Kafka clusters usually have multiple brokers.
		
		8. Cluster: Kafka Cluster is a group of Kafka brokers(servers) working together to manage and distribute data via topics. A Cluster is a collection of brokers.
		
		9. ZooKeeper: ZooKeeper was used for managing Kafka’s cluster metadata and coordination. Starting from Kafka version 3.0, it is optional and can be replaced with Kafka’s built-in KRaft mode.


		```
		+-----------+          +--------+          +----------------+
		| Producer  |  ----->  | Topic  |  ----->  | Partition(s)   |
		+-----------+          +--------+          +----------------+
													   |
													   v
												  +---------+
												  | Offset  |
												  +---------+

														|
														v
		+------------+       +----------------+     +-------------+
		| Consumer   | <---- | Consumer Group | <-- | Partition(s)|
		+------------+       +----------------+     +-------------+

														^
														|
												 +--------------+
												 | Kafka Broker |
												 +--------------+

														^
														|
												 +--------------+
												 | Kafka Cluster|
												 +--------------+

														^
														|
								 +------------------------------------------+
								 | ZooKeeper (or KRaft in Kafka 3.0+)       |
								 +------------------------------------------+
		```
		
		This diagram outlines the flow from **Producer** to **Topic**, how data is stored in **Partitions** with **Offsets**, how **Consumers** (in **Consumer Groups**) read that data, and how everything is managed by **Brokers** in a **Cluster**, coordinated via **ZooKeeper or KRaft**.
		
		Example: Kafka in Uber-like Ride-Hailing System
		------------------------------------------------
		🧑 Rider (Uber App):
		Opens the app and requests a ride.

		Location data, ride request, and preferences are sent as events/messages to Kafka.

		🔄 Kafka - Data Flow:
		Producer: Rider app sends data to Kafka topic (e.g., ride-requests).

		Topic & Partition: Kafka stores the message in a topic partition.

		Broker: Kafka broker holds this message and makes it available.

		Consumer Group (Ride Matching Service):

		A backend service consuming from `ride-requests` topic.

		Matches the rider with nearby drivers.

		🚗 Driver (Uber Driver App):
		Driver app receives ride request pushed via Kafka (e.g., topic driver-notifications).

		Kafka pushes events to notify drivers in real-time (e.g., "New ride request", "Rider location updated").

		| Kafka Topic     | Data Handled                                         |
		| --------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- |
		| `ride-requests` | Ride requests from riders                            |
		| `driver-status` | Driver's live location and availability              |
		| `trip-status`   | Updates like "ride accepted", "arrived", "completed" |
		| `notifications` | Alerts and updates for riders and drivers            |


		Example : how driver moving location works using kafka
		--------------------------------------------
		high-level flow

		1. Driver's mobile app collects GPS data (latitude, longitude, timestamp) every few seconds
		2. The app sends this data to a backend REST API or WebSocket endpoint
		3. The backend service publishes this location update to a Kafka topic called `driver-location`
		4. Multiple services (e.g., rider tracking service, ETA calculator, maps engine) consume messages from `driver-location` topic
		5. Kafka ensures real-time, fault-tolerant, and scalable delivery of these updates

		example kafka topic and message

		* topic: `driver-location`
		* message:

		  ```
		  {
			"driverId": "D123",
			"latitude": 17.385044,
			"longitude": 78.486671,
			"timestamp": "2025-05-07T10:15:30Z"
		  }
		  ```

		consumer services reading from `driver-location`

		* Rider UI backend: updates rider app with real-time map
		* ETA Service: recalculates estimated arrival time
		* Surge Pricing Engine: adjusts pricing in high-demand zones
		* Analytics: stores historical movement for trends or fraud detection

		how kafka helps

		* handles millions of updates per second
		* decouples producers (driver app) from consumers (rider UI, ETA, analytics)
		* ensures messages are durable and can be replayed if needed


		EXAMPLE TO IMPLEMENT WITH KAFKA
		================================

		---

		## ✅ **Use Case: Ride Booking System (Rider-Driver App)**

		### 🎯 **Scenario:**

		A **Rider** requests a ride from the Rider app. This request is published to a **Kafka topic**. All **available Drivers** are subscribed to this topic. A **Driver** accepts the ride, and the match is confirmed via another topic.

		---

		### ⚙️ **Architecture Overview:**

		```
		[Rider Service] ---> [Kafka Topic: ride-requests] ---> [Driver Service]
		   |                                                         |
		   |<--- Kafka Topic: ride-matches <---[Driver Accepts Ride]-|
		```

		---

		## 🔧 Microservices Involved:

		### 1. 🚗 **Rider Service**

		* Exposes REST APIs to request a ride.
		* Publishes ride requests to Kafka topic: `ride-requests`.

		### 2. 🧍‍♂️ **Driver Service**

		* Subscribes to `ride-requests` Kafka topic.
		* On matching, publishes to `ride-matches` topic.
		* Optional: stores matched rides in DB (MongoDB/MySQL).

		---

		## 📦 Kafka Topics:

		| Topic Name      | Purpose                                 |
		| --------------- | --------------------------------------- |
		| `ride-requests` | Rider requests a ride                   |
		| `ride-matches`  | Driver accepts and matches with a Rider |

		---

		## 🔄 Sample Flow:

		1. Rider sends `POST /request-ride` → `RiderService`
		2. `RiderService` → Publishes message to Kafka topic `ride-requests`
		3. `DriverService` (Kafka consumer) reads the message, decides to accept
		4. `DriverService` publishes confirmation to `ride-matches`
		5. `RiderService` listens on `ride-matches` topic to notify the Rider

		---

		## 🧪 Sample Payloads:

		### 🛎️ Rider → Kafka (ride-requests)

		```json
		{
		  "riderId": "rider123",
		  "pickup": "Hyderabad",
		  "drop": "Secunderabad",
		  "timestamp": "2025-05-07T10:00:00"
		}
		```

		### 🧑‍✈️ Driver → Kafka (ride-matches)

		```json
		{
		  "driverId": "driver456",
		  "riderId": "rider123",
		  "vehicle": "TS09AB1234",
		  "eta": 5
		}
		```

		---

		## 🧩 Tech Stack

		* Spring Boot (REST APIs)
		* Apache Kafka (Messaging)
		* Spring Kafka
		* MongoDB / MySQL (optional for persistence)
		* Docker (containerized microservices)
		* Swagger (API documentation)

		---

		## 📁 Folder Structure (Simplified)

		```
		ride-booking-app/
		├── rider-service/
		│   └── RideController.java
		│   └── KafkaProducerService.java
		│   └── KafkaConsumerService.java
		├── driver-service/
		│   └── DriverMatchConsumer.java
		│   └── RideMatchProducer.java
		└── common/
			└── RideRequestDTO.java
			└── RideMatchDTO.java
		```

		---

		Would you like:

		* ✅ **Code for these services**?
		* 📄 **API documentation (Swagger)**?
		* 🐳 **Docker + Kafka setup (docker-compose)**?
		* 📦 **Downloadable project structure**?



		---

		### ✅ **Code for Rider and Driver Services using Spring Boot + Kafka**

		#### 1. **Rider Service**

		The **Rider Service** will be responsible for sending ride requests to Kafka.

		**`RiderController.java`**

		```java
		@RestController
		@RequestMapping("/rider")
		public class RiderController {

			@Autowired
			private KafkaProducerService kafkaProducerService;

			@PostMapping("/request-ride")
			public ResponseEntity<String> requestRide(@RequestBody RideRequest rideRequest) {
				kafkaProducerService.sendRideRequest(rideRequest);
				return ResponseEntity.ok("Ride request sent successfully!");
			}
		}
		```

		**`KafkaProducerService.java`**

		```java
		@Service
		public class KafkaProducerService {

			private static final String TOPIC = "ride-requests";

			@Autowired
			private KafkaTemplate<String, RideRequest> kafkaTemplate;

			public void sendRideRequest(RideRequest rideRequest) {
				kafkaTemplate.send(TOPIC, rideRequest);
			}
		}
		```

		**`RideRequest.java`**

		```java
		public class RideRequest {
			private String riderId;
			private String pickupLocation;
			private String dropLocation;
			private String timestamp;

			// Getters and Setters
		}
		```

		#### 2. **Driver Service**

		The **Driver Service** will consume ride requests from Kafka.

		**`DriverMatchConsumer.java`**

		```java
		@Service
		public class DriverMatchConsumer {

			@KafkaListener(topics = "ride-requests", groupId = "driver-group")
			public void consume(RideRequest rideRequest) {
				System.out.println("Received ride request: " + rideRequest);
				// Simulate accepting a ride
				RideMatch rideMatch = new RideMatch(rideRequest.getRiderId(), "driver456", "TS09AB1234", 5);
				// Send match back to Kafka
				kafkaMatchProducer.sendRideMatch(rideMatch);
			}
		}
		```

		**`KafkaMatchProducer.java`**

		```java
		@Service
		public class KafkaMatchProducer {

			private static final String TOPIC = "ride-matches";

			@Autowired
			private KafkaTemplate<String, RideMatch> kafkaTemplate;

			public void sendRideMatch(RideMatch rideMatch) {
				kafkaTemplate.send(TOPIC, rideMatch);
			}
		}
		```

		**`RideMatch.java`**

		```java
		public class RideMatch {
			private String riderId;
			private String driverId;
			private String vehicle;
			private int eta;

			// Getters and Setters
		}
			```

		Great question! After the `KafkaMatchProducer` sends the ride match information to the `ride-matches` Kafka topic, the **Rider Service** (or another system component) should listen to this topic and notify the rider accordingly. Here's how it can continue:

		### 1. **Rider Service listens to the `ride-matches` topic:**

		To notify the rider, the **Rider Service** needs to consume the ride match message from Kafka. It will listen to the `ride-matches` topic, process the ride match data, and then notify the rider through an appropriate channel (such as a push notification, email, or SMS).

		### 2. **Kafka Consumer in Rider Service**

		The **Rider Service** will have a Kafka consumer that listens to the `ride-matches` topic for any ride match updates (like a driver accepting the ride). Once a message is received, the service can trigger a notification for the rider.

		**`RiderMatchConsumer.java`**

		```java
		@Service
		public class RiderMatchConsumer {

			@Autowired
			private NotificationService notificationService;

			@KafkaListener(topics = "ride-matches", groupId = "rider-group")
			public void consumeRideMatch(RideMatch rideMatch) {
				// Logic to handle the ride match (e.g., send a notification to the rider)
				System.out.println("Ride matched with driver: " + rideMatch.getDriverId());

				// Notify the rider (this could be via email, SMS, or in-app push notification)
				String message = "Your ride has been matched with driver " + rideMatch.getDriverId() +
								 " in vehicle " + rideMatch.getVehicle() +
								 ". Estimated time of arrival: " + rideMatch.getEta() + " minutes.";
				notificationService.sendNotification(rideMatch.getRiderId(), message);
			}
		}
		```

		### 3. **NotificationService to Notify the Rider**

		You'll need a `NotificationService` that handles the actual notification to the rider. It could use any notification system (like email, SMS, or an in-app push notification service).

		**`NotificationService.java`**

		```java
		@Service
		public class NotificationService {

			public void sendNotification(String riderId, String message) {
				// Simulating sending a notification (in reality, you could use email, SMS, or push notifications)
				System.out.println("Notifying rider " + riderId + ": " + message);
			}
		}
		```

		### 4. **End-to-End Flow**

		Here’s the end-to-end flow:

		1. **Rider requests a ride** via the `RiderService`, which sends the ride request to the `ride-requests` Kafka topic.
		2. **Driver accepts the ride** in the `DriverService`, which consumes from the `ride-requests` topic and sends the ride match details to the `ride-matches` Kafka topic.
		3. **RiderService listens** to the `ride-matches` topic using `RiderMatchConsumer`. When it receives the ride match information, it triggers the `NotificationService` to send a message to the rider.

		### 5. **Rider is notified:**

		* Once the rider match is confirmed, the `NotificationService` sends a message to the rider (this could be via a push notification or any other notification system you use).

		---

		### Additional Details for Notification:

		You could integrate an actual notification service such as:

		* **Push Notifications**: Using Firebase or other platforms like OneSignal.
		* **SMS**: Using an API like Twilio.
		* **Email**: Using an SMTP service or something like SendGrid.

		This ensures that as soon as a driver accepts the ride, the rider is notified in real-time.

		---

		### 📄 **API Documentation (Swagger)**

		To generate API documentation using Swagger, add the following dependencies to your `pom.xml`:

		```xml
		<dependency>
			<groupId>io.springfox</groupId>
			<artifactId>springfox-boot-starter</artifactId>
			<version>3.0.0</version>
		</dependency>
		```

		Then, add a configuration class to enable Swagger:

		**`SwaggerConfig.java`**

		```java
		@Configuration
		@EnableOpenApi
		public class SwaggerConfig {
		}
		```

		This will automatically generate the Swagger UI at `http://localhost:8080/swagger-ui/`.

		---

		### 🐳 **Docker + Kafka Setup (docker-compose)**

		**docker-compose.yml**

		```yaml
		version: '3.8'
		services:
		  zookeeper:
			image: wurstmeister/zookeeper:3.4.6
			ports:
			  - "2181:2181"
		  kafka:
			image: wurstmeister/kafka:latest
			ports:
			  - "9093:9093"
			environment:
			  KAFKA_ADVERTISED_LISTENER: INSIDE_KAFKA:9093
			  KAFKA_LISTENER_SECURITY_PROTOCOL: PLAINTEXT
			  KAFKA_LISTENER_NAME: INSIDE_KAFKA
			  KAFKA_LISTENERS: INSIDE_KAFKA://kafka:9093
			  KAFKA_LISTENER_PORT: 9093
			  KAFKA_LISTENER_NAME_INTERNAL: INSIDE_KAFKA
			  KAFKA_ZOOKEEPER_CONNECT: zookeeper:2181
			  KAFKA_LISTENER_INTERCEPTOR_CLASSES: io.confluent.kafka.streams.serdes.avro.SchemaRegistryInterceptor
			depends_on:
			  - zookeeper
		```

		---

		### 🎯 **Run Your Setup**

		1. **Build and run the services**:

		   * `docker-compose up -d` (This will start Zookeeper and Kafka).
		   * Build and run the Spring Boot services:

			 * `mvn clean install`
			 * `mvn spring-boot:run`
		2. **Testing**:

		   * Use a tool like **Postman** to send a `POST` request to `http://localhost:8080/rider/request-ride` with a sample JSON body:

			 ```json
			 {
			   "riderId": "rider123",
			   "pickupLocation": "Hyderabad",
			   "dropLocation": "Secunderabad",
			   "timestamp": "2025-05-07T10:00:00"
			 }
			 ```
		   * Check the Kafka topic to ensure the message is properly sent and consumed.

		---

		===========================================================================================================================================================================================
		To build a full application for the ride-hailing system, here's an overview of the flow and the technologies involved:

		### **Overview**

		* **Rider:** Requests a ride from a pickup location.
		* **Driver:** Accepts the ride request and moves towards the pickup location.
		* **Notification:** Rider gets notified with the driver's status (moving towards pickup).
		* **Pin Confirmation:** Rider confirms upon reaching the driver for the ride to start.
		* **Drop Location:** Once the ride is completed, the driver drops the rider at the destination.

		### **Technologies to Use**

		1. **Kafka** - For messaging between the Rider and Driver, and managing events in real-time (e.g., ride request, status updates).
		2. **Spring Boot** - Backend API for handling ride requests, notifications, and ride status.
		3. **MongoDB** - Store user profiles, ride requests, ride history, and other data.
		4. **Angular** - Frontend application for the Rider and Driver to interact with.
		5. **Spring Security** (Optional) - For securing endpoints, such as for user authentication (Rider/Driver).

		### **Steps to Implement**

		1. **Set up Kafka locally:**

		   * Install [Kafka](https://kafka.apache.org/quickstart) and run it locally. Kafka requires Zookeeper, so you need both Kafka and Zookeeper running for it to function.
		   * You can follow the instructions from the Kafka Quickstart guide for local installation.

		2. **Backend (Spring Boot + Kafka + MongoDB):**

		   * Set up a Spring Boot project with dependencies:

			 * **Kafka**: `spring-kafka`
			 * **MongoDB**: `spring-boot-starter-data-mongodb`
			 * **Spring Boot Starter Web** for creating REST APIs
			 * **Spring Boot Starter Security** (optional)
		   * Implement the following key components:

			 * **Kafka Producer and Consumer**: For sending and receiving messages (e.g., for ride requests, status updates).
			 * **Ride Service**: For handling ride requests and status changes.
			 * **Rider and Driver Services**: For managing user information.
			 * **MongoDB repositories** for storing user and ride data.
			 * **Notifications**: Notify the Rider when the driver is en route.

		3. **Frontend (Angular):**

		   * Create an Angular project with components for:

			 * **Rider**: Request a ride, confirm pickup, view ride status.
			 * **Driver**: Accept the ride, update status, and complete the ride.
		   * Use **RxJS** and **HTTP Client** for making API calls to the Spring Boot backend.

		4. **Kafka Message Flow**:

		   * When a rider requests a ride, a Kafka message is sent to a Kafka topic.
		   * The driver listens for ride requests via Kafka, and when a match is found, it sends the driver’s status back to the rider.
		   * Status updates (e.g., driver on the way, at the pickup location, ride started, etc.) are exchanged via Kafka.

		5. **Pin Confirmation**:

		   * Implement a feature where, upon reaching the rider, the driver requests a pin confirmation from the rider to start the ride. The rider confirms the pin before the ride begins.

		6. **End Ride**:

		   * When the driver reaches the drop location, the ride is marked as completed.

		---

		### **Backend (Spring Boot)**

		Here’s an example of how to configure the backend:

		#### **1. Dependencies (pom.xml)**

		```xml
		<dependencies>
			<!-- Kafka dependencies -->
			<dependency>
				<groupId>org.springframework.kafka</groupId>
				<artifactId>spring-kafka</artifactId>
			</dependency>
			
			<!-- MongoDB dependencies -->
			<dependency>
				<groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
				<artifactId>spring-boot-starter-data-mongodb</artifactId>
			</dependency>
			
			<!-- Spring Web for RESTful APIs -->
			<dependency>
				<groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
				<artifactId>spring-boot-starter-web</artifactId>
			</dependency>

			<!-- Spring Boot Security (optional) -->
			<dependency>
				<groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
				<artifactId>spring-boot-starter-security</artifactId>
			</dependency>

			<!-- Kafka and MongoDB Integration -->
			<dependency>
				<groupId>org.springframework.kafka</groupId>
				<artifactId>spring-kafka</artifactId>
			</dependency>
		</dependencies>
		```

		#### **2. Kafka Configuration**

		**Kafka Producer and Consumer Config:**

		```java
		@Configuration
		public class KafkaConfig {

			@Bean
			public ProducerFactory<String, String> producerFactory() {
				return new DefaultKafkaProducerFactory<>(producerConfigs());
			}

			@Bean
			public ConsumerFactory<String, String> consumerFactory() {
				return new DefaultKafkaConsumerFactory<>(consumerConfigs());
			}

			@Bean
			public KafkaListenerContainerFactory<ConcurrentMessageListenerContainer<String, String>> kafkaListenerContainerFactory() {
				ConcurrentMessageListenerContainerFactory<String, String> factory =
						new ConcurrentMessageListenerContainerFactory<>();
				factory.setConsumerFactory(consumerFactory());
				return factory;
			}
		}
		```

		#### **3. Ride Service**

		```java
		@Service
		public class RideService {

			@Autowired
			private KafkaTemplate<String, String> kafkaTemplate;

			@Autowired
			private RideRepository rideRepository;

			public void requestRide(RideRequest request) {
				// Store ride request in MongoDB
				Ride ride = new Ride(request.getRiderId(), request.getPickupLocation(), "Pending");
				rideRepository.save(ride);

				// Send Kafka message about the ride request
				kafkaTemplate.send("ride-requests", "Ride Request: " + ride.getId());
			}

			// Handle driver's acceptance and other ride events
			public void handleDriverAcceptance(String driverId, String rideId) {
				// Send Kafka message for ride acceptance
				kafkaTemplate.send("ride-status", "Driver " + driverId + " accepted ride " + rideId);
			}

			// Notify rider when the driver is en route
			public void notifyRider(String rideId, String status) {
				kafkaTemplate.send("ride-status", "Driver is " + status + " for ride " + rideId);
			}
		}
		```

		#### **4. MongoDB Repository**

		```java
		@Repository
		public interface RideRepository extends MongoRepository<Ride, String> {
			List<Ride> findByRiderId(String riderId);
		}
		```

		### **Frontend (Angular)**

		Create the following components:

		* **RideRequestComponent**: Rider can request a ride.
		* **RideStatusComponent**: Rider can see the current ride status.
		* **DriverComponent**: Driver can accept a ride request and start the ride.

		Use **Angular services** to communicate with the backend via HTTP and use **RxJS** to handle asynchronous data.

		#### **1. Ride Request Component**

		```typescript
		@Component({
		  selector: 'app-ride-request',
		  templateUrl: './ride-request.component.html',
		  styleUrls: ['./ride-request.component.css']
		})
		export class RideRequestComponent {

		  constructor(private rideService: RideService) { }

		  requestRide() {
			this.rideService.requestRide({ pickupLocation: 'XYZ' }).subscribe(response => {
			  console.log('Ride requested successfully');
			});
		  }
		}
		```

		#### **2. Ride Status Service**

		```typescript
		@Injectable({
		  providedIn: 'root'
		})
		export class RideService {

		  private apiUrl = 'http://localhost:8080/api/rides';  // Backend API URL

		  constructor(private http: HttpClient) { }

		  requestRide(request: any): Observable<any> {
			return this.http.post(`${this.apiUrl}/request`, request);
		  }

		  getRideStatus(rideId: string): Observable<any> {
			return this.http.get(`${this.apiUrl}/${rideId}/status`);
		  }
		}
		```

		### **Deployment & Setup**

		1. **Kafka Setup**: Follow Kafka's installation guide to set up Kafka and Zookeeper locally.
		2. **MongoDB Setup**: Install MongoDB locally or use a cloud service like MongoDB Atlas.
		3. **Spring Boot Application**: Run your Spring Boot application locally.
		4. **Angular App**: Run your Angular frontend locally.
		5. **Docker & Kubernetes**: You can dockerize both Spring Boot and Angular applications and deploy them in Kubernetes for production environments.

		### **Local Installation Checklist**

		1. **Kafka**: Make sure Kafka and Zookeeper are running.
		2. **MongoDB**: Ensure MongoDB is set up locally or connected to a cloud instance.
		3. **Spring Boot**: Run Spring Boot with proper Kafka and MongoDB configurations.
		4. **Angular**: Ensure the Angular app is built and running with live updates.

		This approach provides the full architecture to handle ride requests, notifications, and ride status with Kafka, Spring Boot, Angular, and MongoDB.
		===================================================================================================================================================================================


		Here's a clear explanation of how the **Uber-like Kafka-based communication flow** works between the **Rider App**, **Driver App**, and **Matching Service**, based on the Spring Boot implementation I provided:

		---

		## 🛤️ **Step-by-Step Flow Explanation**

		### 🔁 1. **Rider App Requests a Ride**

		* Endpoint: `POST /rider/request`
		* Sends a `RideRequest` JSON to Kafka topic `ride_requests`.
		* Kafka acts as a message broker and stores this message temporarily.

		**Example message:**

		```json
		{
		  "riderId": "R123",
		  "pickup": "37.7749,-122.4194",
		  "destination": "37.8044,-122.2711",
		  "timestamp": "2025-05-12T10:30:00Z"
		}
		```

		---

		### 🚗 2. **Driver Marks Themselves Available**

		* Endpoint: `POST /driver/available`
		* Sends a `DriverAvailability` JSON to Kafka topic `available_drivers`.

		**Example message:**

		```json
		{
		  "driverId": "D456",
		  "location": "37.7750,-122.4183",
		  "status": "available",
		  "timestamp": "2025-05-12T10:30:05Z"
		}
		```

		---

		### 🧠 3. **Matching Service Matches Rider with Driver**

		* The `MatchingService` is a Kafka consumer that:

		  * Listens to both `ride_requests` and `available_drivers` topics.
		  * Maintains in-memory queues for riders and available drivers.
		* When both queues have entries, it **matches** one rider to one driver.
		* It then **produces a `RideAssignment`** to the `ride_assignments` topic.

		**Example `RideAssignment`:**

		```json
		{
		  "rideId": "RID789",
		  "riderId": "R123",
		  "driverId": "D456",
		  "pickup": "37.7749,-122.4194",
		  "eta": "5 mins"
		}
		```

		---

		### 📲 4. **Driver Receives the Assignment**

		* The `DriverController` listens (as a consumer) to the `ride_assignments` topic.
		* It receives the `RideAssignment` message and logs or processes it.
		* This tells the driver who the rider is, where to go, etc.

		---

		### 🚕 5. **Driver Sends Ride Updates**

		* Endpoint: `POST /driver/update`
		* Sends `RideUpdate` messages to Kafka topic `ride_updates` with current ride status.

		**Example:**

		```json
		{
		  "rideId": "RID789",
		  "driverId": "D456",
		  "status": "driver_arrived",
		  "location": "37.7752,-122.4180"
		}
		```

		---

		### 👀 6. **(Optional) Rider App Subscribes to `ride_updates`**

		* A consumer in the Rider App can listen to `ride_updates` topic.
		* This allows real-time updates like "Driver has arrived", "Trip started", etc.

		---

		## 🎯 Summary of Kafka Topics and Their Roles

		| Kafka Topic         | Who Produces?    | Who Consumes?        | Purpose                            |
		| ------------------- | ---------------- | -------------------- | ---------------------------------- |
		| `ride_requests`     | Rider App        | Matching Service     | New ride request by rider          |
		| `available_drivers` | Driver App       | Matching Service     | Driver is available for assignment |
		| `ride_assignments`  | Matching Service | Driver App           | Assigns rider to driver            |
		| `ride_updates`      | Driver App       | Rider App (optional) | Status updates from driver         |

		---

		Would you like a **sequence diagram** to visualize this flow?

		EMPLOYEE CAB SERVICE
		=======================

		To expand your **Employee Management (Monolithic) Spring Boot application** by adding a **Cab Booking Service** and integrating it with **Apache Kafka** and **MongoDB**, here’s a step-by-step guide for both setup and implementation.

		---

		## ✅ OVERVIEW: What You Will Build

		You'll extend your app to:

		1. Keep **Employee** functionality as it is.
		2. Add **Cab Booking functionality** (for employees).
		3. Use **Apache Kafka** for messaging between Employee and Cab services.
		4. Use **MongoDB** to store Cab Booking details.

		---

		## ⚙️ TOOLS & TECHNOLOGIES

		* **Spring Boot**
		* **Spring Data JPA** (for Employee + MySQL)
		* **Spring Data MongoDB** (for CabBooking)
		* **Apache Kafka**
		* **MongoDB**
		* **Lombok, Jackson, etc.**

		---

		## 🔧 STEP 1: Install Kafka & Zookeeper Locally

		If you don’t have Kafka, follow these instructions:

		### ✅ Install Kafka

		1. **Download Kafka** from Apache:
		   [https://kafka.apache.org/downloads](https://kafka.apache.org/downloads)

		2. **Extract it** and go to the folder.

		3. Start **Zookeeper**:

		```bash
		bin/zookeeper-server-start.sh config/zookeeper.properties
		```

		4. Start **Kafka Server**:

		```bash
		bin/kafka-server-start.sh config/server.properties
		```

		---

		## 🔧 STEP 2: Install MongoDB (If not installed)

		### ✅ Option 1: Install MongoDB locally

		Use: [https://www.mongodb.com/try/download/community](https://www.mongodb.com/try/download/community)

		Start with:

		```bash
		mongod
		```

		### ✅ Option 2: Use MongoDB Atlas (Cloud):

		[https://www.mongodb.com/cloud/atlas/register](https://www.mongodb.com/cloud/atlas/register)

		---

		## 🧱 STEP 3: Project Structure

		```plaintext
		employee-management/
		├── controller/
		│   ├── EmployeeController.java
		│   └── CabBookingController.java
		├── entity/
		│   ├── Employee.java (MySQL)
		│   └── CabBooking.java (MongoDB)
		├── repository/
		│   ├── EmployeeRepository.java
		│   └── CabBookingRepository.java
		├── service/
		│   ├── EmployeeService.java
		│   └── CabBookingService.java
		├── kafka/
		│   ├── CabBookingProducer.java
		│   └── CabBookingConsumer.java
		├── config/
		│   └── KafkaConfig.java
		└── application.properties
		```

		---

		## 📦 STEP 4: Maven Dependencies (`pom.xml`)

		```xml
			<!-- Spring Boot -->
			<dependency>

				<artifactId>spring-boot-starter-web</artifactId>
			</dependency>

			<!-- JPA & MySQL -->
		```

		```properties
		spring.datasource.username=root
		spring.datasource.password=root

		spring.kafka.consumer.auto-offset-reset=earliest
		spring.kafka.consumer.key-deserializer=org.apache.kafka.common.serialization.StringDeserializer
		spring.kafka.consumer.value-deserializer=org.apache.kafka.common.serialization.StringDeserializer
		spring.kafka.producer.key-serializer=org.apache.kafka.common.serialization.StringSerializer
		spring.kafka.producer.value-serializer=org.apache.kafka.common.serialization.StringSerializer
		```

		## 🧾 STEP 6: Entity Classes

		### 1. **Employee.java** (MySQL)

		```java
			@GeneratedValue
			private Long id;

			private String name;
			private String department;
		}
		```

		### 2. **CabBooking.java** (MongoDB)

		```java
		@Document(collection = "cab_bookings")
		@Data
		public class CabBooking {
			@Id
			private String id;
			private Long employeeId;
			private String pickupLocation;
			private String dropLocation;
			private LocalDateTime bookingTime;
		}
		```

		## 💾 STEP 7: Repositories

		### 1. **EmployeeRepository**

		```java
		public interface EmployeeRepository extends JpaRepository<Employee, Long> {}
		```

		### 2. **CabBookingRepository**

		```java

		```

		## 🔄 STEP 8: Kafka Config & Messaging

		### **KafkaConfig.java**

		```java
		@Configuration
		public class KafkaConfig {
			@Bean
			public ProducerFactory<String, String> producerFactory() {
				return new DefaultKafkaProducerFactory<>(Map.of(
					ProducerConfig.BOOTSTRAP_SERVERS_CONFIG, "localhost:9092",
					ProducerConfig.KEY_SERIALIZER_CLASS_CONFIG, StringSerializer.class,
					ProducerConfig.VALUE_SERIALIZER_CLASS_CONFIG, StringSerializer.class
				));
			}

			@Bean
			public KafkaTemplate<String, String> kafkaTemplate() {
				return new KafkaTemplate<>(producerFactory());
			}
		}
		```

		### **CabBookingProducer.java**

		```java
		@Component
		@RequiredArgsConstructor
		public class CabBookingProducer {
			private final KafkaTemplate<String, String> kafkaTemplate;

			public void sendBookingRequest(CabBooking booking) throws JsonProcessingException {
				ObjectMapper mapper = new ObjectMapper();
				String msg = mapper.writeValueAsString(booking);
				kafkaTemplate.send("cab_booking_topic", msg);
			}
		}
		```

		### **CabBookingConsumer.java**

		```java
		@Component
		@RequiredArgsConstructor
		public class CabBookingConsumer {

			private final CabBookingRepository cabBookingRepository;

			@KafkaListener(topics = "cab_booking_topic", groupId = "cab_group")
			public void consume(String message) throws JsonProcessingException {
				ObjectMapper mapper = new ObjectMapper();
				CabBooking booking = mapper.readValue(message, CabBooking.class);
				booking.setBookingTime(LocalDateTime.now());
				cabBookingRepository.save(booking);
			}
		}
		```

		---

		### **EmployeeService.java**

		```java
		@Service
		```

		### **CabBookingService.java**

		```java
		@Service
		@RequiredArgsConstructor
		public class CabBookingService {
			private final CabBookingProducer producer;

			public String bookCab(CabBooking booking) throws JsonProcessingException {
				producer.sendBookingRequest(booking);
				return "Booking initiated!";
			}
		}
		```
